# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

# 6 C&Q Planning

# 6.1 Introduction

C&Q is a planned, documented, and managed engineering activity that starts with the establishment of the URS and continues to start-up and acceptance and release of systems. A well-planned program is essential and leads to an efficient, “right the first time”, and cost-effective C&Q effort; the result is the delivery of systems that operate in a safe manner, meet established design requirements and stakeholder expectations, and are suitable for the intended purpose. Planning provides the ties between process understanding and system design; it focuses qualification efforts to enhance the likelihood that the system will deliver quality products while maintaining a controlled operating state. It also acts as a means for communication between members of the multidisciplinary project team.

This chapter focuses on the formal C&Q Plan. There are other plans that can be generated, such as overall project plans or cleanliness plans, which have great value but are not covered in this chapter. Refer to Chapter 21 (Appendix 7) for a listing of supporting plans and general concepts for each. Regulatory expectations for C&Q planning are described in Chapter 15 (Appendix 1).

# 6.1.1 Purpose and Benefits

A well-conceived and executed C&Q Plan can greatly facilitate a timely and cost-effective qualification effort. It is essential to have excellent communication, planning, and coordination between the Quality Unit, vendors, operations, automation, engineering, construction, commissioning, and qualification personnel, to name a few, to deliver a project on time, within budget, and meeting all expectations. A C&Q plan facilitates this communication.

An important secondary goal of the C&Q Plan is to foster an interdisciplinary team approach to C&Q. Well-planned project logistics are crucial to the success of every project. A well-coordinated effort leads to a high quality, effective, and efficient C&Q project.

A single, integrated, overall schedule should be developed with input from the entire project team. This schedule should cover all systems (utilizing defined system boundaries as discussed in Section 3.2) and phases of the projects lifecycle from construction to Process Validation/PPQ completion. This schedule should be updated and issued regularly. These updates should not drive a C&Q Plan revision, but the C&Q Plan should identify how the schedule updates will be captured and communicated.

An integrated schedule, using a critical path method, can increase the awareness of dependent activities and provide a clear understanding of the critical path. Including all project team members in the schedule development will engage the team members and foster better planning. All team members should comprehend the schedule format.

Ordering long delivery time items early can help timelines, but consideration should be made to ensure the process requirements for those items are understood and included in the requisition. Understanding when to engage support for C&Q helps to support the success of the project.

A well-planned effort can ensure that the user, business, regulatory, and patient expectations are met. All efforts to ensure the system is suitable for the intended purpose (e.g., start-up, balancing, trial runs, establishing maintenance programs) should be included in the planning phase since this is where the formality of the documentation can be established. These expectations include:

- Systems are delivered on time and within budget
- Systems meet approved user requirements and are suitable for the intended purpose
- Stakeholder needs, including speed to market, are met

For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
---
# ISPE Baseline® Guide: Commissioning and Qualification

• Products produced from the systems meet defined quality standards

# 6.2 C&Q Planning Approach

The amount of planning, documentation, reporting, and level of detail of the C&Q Plan should be based on expectations of the organization/site, size and complexity of the system or project, and the potential impact on product quality and patient safety.

A C&Q Plan should not repeat details that are in other documents but needs to capture the organization of those documents in order to facilitate an efficient project execution.

A separate C&Q Plan is not always necessary. Aspects of C&Q planning can be included with other documents, such as a validation plan or change control. For example, the simple change of adding a small system would not need a separate C&Q Plan; the requirements could be incorporated into a change control plan. More complex systems would benefit from a separate C&Q Plan, for example, the installation of a filling line with terminal sterilization and secondary packaging or the doubling in capacity of a manufacturing site including additional utilities to support the expansion.

# 6.2.1 Timing of C&Q Plan

In an overall scheme, the creation of the C&Q Plan falls in the process map as shown in Figure 1.1 (Chapter 1). It is important to note that C&Q planning begins prior to the creation of any formal written plans. C&Q planning starts during the planning and design phase of a project and continues through project implementation. A documented strategy may be developed as a precursor to a C&Q Plan, to help align team members with the process and personnel who will be responsible for C&Q.

A C&Q Plan should begin early in a project in the concept or early basic engineering phase.

The C&Q Plan needs to be approved before the respective testing documents (i.e., test instructions) are approved, and such test instructions need to be approved before test execution starts to support qualification. This is recommended for commissioning plans and test instructions. The C&Q Plan should address how existing test documentation and test results from commissioning (including FAT, SAT, etc.) are referenced in qualification documents, as appropriate.

# 6.2.2 Lifecycle of C&Q Plan

The C&Q Plan is not finalized until after the risk management process has reached a point to capture the impact of product quality decisions or of establishing the CDEs.

C&Q Plans are expected to be kept up-to-date as changes to the project are made. Revisions of the C&Q Plan may be necessary as new information is learned or as modifications to the design are made, e.g., changes in scope (additions or removals), timing, or identification of additional CAs/CDEs.

Approval is recommended for these early plans and for subsequent changes made by the revision process; this ensures agreement at various stages of the C&Q approach. Changes to the C&Q Plan should be reviewed and approved by the original approvers (or their designate) and a revision history should be maintained of significant changes to the Plan.

It should be noted that revisions may not always be necessary. Minor departures from the C&Q Plan may be documented in the Qualification Summary Report with appropriate justification and rationale, as deemed appropriate by the project team.

For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
---
# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

At the end of a project, a written document capturing the completion and closure of the project should be created. See Chapter 8 for more details regarding acceptance and release. Changes to the C&Q Plan should be handled under change management (as discussed in Chapter 12) to ensure that as changes are implemented, the impact to C&Q efforts is addressed.

# 6.2.3 Elements to be Addressed

This section is intended to be a discussion of the elements that need to be addressed as part of C&Q planning, not the documents where they are captured.

# 6.2.3.1 Scope of Testing

System classification (as described in Chapter 3) identifies which systems require qualification. The System Risk Assessment (as described in Chapter 4) identifies which elements in those systems require qualification. The URS (as described in Chapter 2) and System Risk Assessment establish the scope of testing for qualification. The equipment specifications establish the scope of testing for commissioning.

The testing approach is based on the most efficient method to test the system and should be captured in the C&Q Plan. If systems are tested separately, the C&Q Plan should include a final test verifying that all systems are able to work together prior to acceptance and release. If PPQ is included in a separate plan, the linkage to the execution of that critical phase should be captured.

# 6.2.3.2 Identification of Document Source

Engineering firms, vendors, contractors, quality, engineering, and owners provide the documentation necessary for design, construction, commissioning, qualification, operation, and technical support of the systems. It is important to identify the documentation source early in the process so the impact on the testing of the system is understood and thus the quality of the documentation understood. This understanding will allow for utilization of the documentation to support qualification.

For example, the decision should be made during planning if any FAT, construction, SAT, or commissioning testing results and documentation will be used to satisfy the requirements to declare the system qualified or suitable for the intended purpose. This decision is based on vendor assessments for C&Q documentation purposes (as described in Chapter 10) and the criticality of the testing. It is often desirable to minimize the amount of retesting performed.

The C&Q Plan may also address the expectations of the documentation. It should be noted that pristine documentation, i.e., documentation that has minimal errors or corrections, is not essential but the documentation needs to be easy to follow and understand. Refer to Chapter 13 for guidance on Good Documentation Practice.

# 6.2.3.3 Coordination with Overall Project Execution Plan

Another important aspect is working with the project manager to ensure that the planning of C&Q activities, whether a separate plan or part of another plan, fits seamlessly into the overall Project Execution Plan. C&Q activities should be properly reflected in the Project Execution Plan at an appropriate level of detail to be able to identify interdependencies between C&Q as well as to other project activities. Commissioning is normally occurring throughout construction and needs to occur when it is appropriate; e.g., commissioning the agitation system cannot be completed until it is energized.

Having these activities in the Project Execution Plan supports:

- Coordination of test activities among C&Q to prepare for FAT or SAT events
- Ability to schedule sequence of testing for multiple systems (e.g., electrical supply before energizing a system)

For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
---
# ISPE Baseline® Guide: Commissioning and Qualification

- Schedule review cycles for qualification documents with Quality Unit representatives

# 6.3 C&Q Plan

The C&Q Plan typically encompasses:

- Description of the scope of the plan (identification of the systems included)
- Description of the C&Q execution strategy
- C&Q related documentation requirements for each identified system (e.g., URS, FAT, SAT, Installation/ Operational Testing, PQ, SOPs, etc.)
- Roles and responsibilities, e.g., approval matrix (see Section 6.4 for example)
- Reference necessary C&Q document templates (e.g., test instructions, test scripts, reports, discrepancy form)
- Planned inspections and tests
- Documentation requirements for the inspection and test results
- Reference procedures for inspections and tests to be performed (as much as possible at this early stage in the project)
- Identification of SMEs for review of the inspection and test results
- For tests to be used to support qualification, include the acceptance criteria to be met for all CDEs (Note: The plan may defer to individual test instructions)

The specific structure and content of the C&Q Plan is typically driven by organization/site requirements.

# 6.3.1 Scope of the Effort

The scope captures all systems that are covered within the scope of the project. This includes both direct impact and not direct impact systems (as described in Chapter 3). More details are provided below.

# 6.3.1.1 Additional Scope Considerations

This Document is licensed to

PQ activities may be included in a C&Q Plan for pharmaceutical grade utilities (e.g., WFI, Purified Water), support systems (e.g., autoclaves, depyrogenation ovens, washers), environments (e.g., non-environmental monitoring in clean rooms), and non-product PQ (e.g., media fills for filling lines). Individual organizations/sites need to decide if these are in qualification or a separate validation program.

Cycle development for areas such as sterilization and cleaning also need to be considered. Cycle development can identify factors that require qualification or that modify the qualification. These are often executed after the equipment qualification effort. It is recommended to not perform the final qualification testing until after cycle development since changes identified by cycle development can be captured in the Engineering Change Management system (see Chapter 12). Individual organizations/sites need to decide if cycle development is part of the C&Q effort and capture the decision in the C&Q Plan.

# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

# 6.3.2 Strategy and Approach

The C&Q Plan needs to define C&Q strategy and approach to aid in overall project planning. The C&Q Plan captures
whether systems will be commissioned only or commissioned and qualified. The system classification (as described
in Chapter 3) and System Risk Assessment (as described in Chapter 4) have identified the CPPs and CDEs that
need to be supported by the C&Q effort.

The rigor of testing depends on the user requirements, CDEs, and system characteristics (e.g., packaged system,
modular, or installed system) as well as experiences with the vendor.

An aspect that needs to be captured in the C&Q Plan is the identification of the documentation source that will be
utilized to satisfy the C&Q requirements. Appropriate prerequisites, means, and checks should be described in the
plan to ensure reliable results, e.g., the pre-approval of vendor/manufacturer documents by an SME. If vendor-
provided documentation or testing is to be accepted to support qualification, the criteria for determining if it can be
utilized needs to be established in the Plan. Refer to Chapter 10 for details regarding vendor assessments for C&Q
documentation purposes.

The C&Q Plan should also capture if individual pieces of equipment or systems will be tested separately because
they are delivered and installed at different times by different vendors or if testing will be executed once all
components are installed. Dependencies within the scope of work (e.g., if one system needs to be tested before
another system is tested) should also be included.

# 6.3.3 Documentation

The C&Q Plan should capture document planning and control as well as execution of testing. The C&Q Plan should
reference appropriate document control and content procedures to satisfy engineering, commissioning, qualification,
operations, and maintenance requirements. Utilizing electronic storage of documents is recommended as it allows for
searching and easier retrieval. If electronic storage will be utilized, ensure the vendors and contracting companies are
aware and can support the delivery of their documentation in that means.

The Plan should define responsibilities for document preparation, collection, review and approval, and how the
documents will be organized. This Guide recommends that a document matrix be considered as a means of planning
and communicating these responsibilities. Documents also need to be tracked to ensure they are completed when
they are needed.

Documents that are typically tracked include:

- URS
- System Risk Assessments
- Drawings
- Equipment specifications including any automation specifications (e.g., functional specifications)
- Vendor correspondence and transmittals
- Requests for information
- Submittals (e.g., shop drawings, manufacturers data sheets, user manuals)
- Required procedures (e.g., equipment operating procedures, maintenance)
- Change management (see Chapter 12)

For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
---
# ISPE Baseline® Guide: Commissioning and Qualification

- Schedules
- Open items/punch list
- Commissioning/qualification execution documents
- Discrepancies
- Turnover packages
- Project reports

# 6.3.4 C&Q Plan Contents

The C&Q Plan should define the systems that will be commissioned and qualified based on the system or area boundaries. The plan should include a description of systems including their means of automation. The system overview should be a high-level process narrative, including system boundaries, based on operational/functional requirements. The C&Q team representatives should provide input in defining system boundaries (which can be in a system description, marked on P&IDs, or highlighted on system drawings) in the early project phases to develop an executable plan. It is often possible to gather much of the system overview information from other project documentation.

The C&Q Plan should address the level of testing for each system. This may range from detailed descriptions to references to standard tests. An alternative is to provide only high-level descriptions in the C&Q Plan and leave the detailed descriptions to execution documents. The selected approach should increase the level of efficiency and effectiveness. The plan should also include project schedules by system (as defined by the system boundaries) that capture the dependencies between systems (i.e., ones that need to be completed before completing others).

Commissioning is an important aspect that requires planning, documentation, and managed resources. Commissioning encompasses testing from FAT, testing during construction, SAT, installation testing, and much of the start-up activities (described in Section 7.3) of a project lifecycle including those that will be used to assess the qualification of a direct impact system. The C&Q Plan should also establish the framework necessary to ensure clear consistent communication and coordination required between predecessor and successor lifecycle activities (e.g., construction, C&Q, Process Validation, operations/maintenance).

Testing during commissioning should be used to support qualification. If commissioning documentation will be used to support qualification, it is critical to involve the leader of the qualification effort and the Quality Unit in the planning and coordination of commissioning activities and deliverables. A periodic commissioning planning and status meeting should be established to ensure appropriate planning for this stage of the project.

The C&Q Plan needs to include C&Q steps. There are two typical phases:

- Installation: Documentation supporting installation is successfully completed. This may include FAT and SAT documentation as well as site commissioning for systems built at the site.
- Operational: Documentation supporting the functionality and performance of the system after the system has been mechanically completed. This may include testing performed in FAT and SAT.

The plan should address the steps to follow when test results do not meet the excepted results or acceptance criteria (discrepancy). There should be a predefined methodology for how discrepancies should be handled (see Section 7.4).

# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

# 7 C&Q Testing and Documentation

# 7.1 Introduction

This chapter provides guidance on C&Q testing and inspection activities and associated documentation for recording
the results of the testing/inspection. The goal of testing and inspection is to confirm that the system has been installed
and operates to meet the requirements.

Although this chapter provides some general guidance on the safety aspects (e.g., pre-start-up checks), the specific
requirements for safety aspects and any local requirements (e.g., signage, access control, equipment protection,
Material Safety Data Sheets) are outside the scope of this Guide. It is the responsibility of the system designer to
produce a system that is compliant with any related local regulations.

# 7.1.1 Approach and Scope

C&Q testing and documentation starts as early as design and ends with the acceptance and release of the system
to the end-user. The process includes planning, document review, and static and dynamic testing of the installation,
function, and performance, including testing of any defined worst-case scenarios.

The requirements and stage of the testing/inspection should be predefined in the planning phase to avoid duplication
of testing. Testing should only be repeated if necessary. For example, retesting may be necessary if there are
changes that may impact a test result, e.g., differing utilities or disconnecting equipment and control systems after
FAT to facilitate shipping.

For systems where the automation is an integral part of the system (typical for manufacturing equipment, e.g., WFI
storage and distribution system with a dedicated control panel), the entire system is tested as an integrated system
comprising the mechanical, electrical, and associated automation.

The scope of testing varies depending on the project scope, but for a new process area would include facilities,
utilities, manufacturing equipment and their associated automation or process control systems.

Testing activities and rigor will depend on the type of system (e.g., manufacturing, automated, utilities), potential to
impact product quality, and on the project (e.g., green field, brown field, relocation, stick-built, modular, off-the-shelf
system).

# 7.1.2 Principles/Concepts

The C&Q testing and documentation approach described in this Guide is based on the following principles:

- Testing is an engineering driven activity that produces the documented evidence that the installation and operation meet the requirements
- Documentation standards need to be predefined (see Chapter 13)
- Training on the documentation standards need to be completed
- Test witness and the associated notification requirements need to be predefined
- Where vendor data will be used to support qualification, a vendor assessment needs to be performed (see Chapter 10)
- System changes need to be managed through Engineering Change Management after approval of the design (see Chapter 12)

For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
---
The process used to manage test failures needs to be predefined (see Section 7.4). GEP should be defined and applied to all phases of C&Q to provide a high degree of assurance that the system meets user requirements while being cost-effective, compliant with regulations, and appropriately documented. Refer to ISPE Good Practice Guide: Good Engineering Practice [22] for additional information.

Organizations/sites may use different terminology and deliverables for C&Q testing/inspection. Table 7.1 provides a comparison of the documentation terminology and Table 7.2 provides example options for inspection/test record documents depending on the system type.

# Table 7.1: C&Q Testing and Inspection – Documentation Terminology Comparison

|Document Type|Description|
|---|---|
|Document Reviews|Confirming that the testing/inspection has been carried out and is recorded per agreed Good Documentation Practices standards (e.g., material certifications, vendor documentation)|
|FAT/SAT|Testing to demonstrate that a system meets established design requirements and contractual obligations …and requires… vendor involvement and technical oversight|
|Testing and Balancing Reports|…|
|Qualification|CAs/CDEs Quality Unit oversight|

# Table 7.2: Example Options for Testing/Inspection Record Documents

|System Type|Option 1|Option 2|
|---|---|---|
|Autoclave|• FAT • SAT • Cycle Development • PQ|• FAT • Installation and Operational Testing Summary • Cycle Development • PQ|
|HVAC (Air Handling Unit up to the final filters)|• Turnover Package • Testing and Balancing Report|• Turnover Package • Commissioning|
|Lyophilization|• FAT • SAT • Cycle Development • PQ|• FAT • Commissioning • Cycle Development • Installation and Operational Testing Summary • PQ|
|Scale|• Receipt Inspection • Calibration Report|• Installation Testing Summary|
|WFI Generation|• FAT • SAT • PQ1|• SAT • PQ1|
|WFI Distribution|• Turnover Package • PQ1*|• Turnover Package • Receipt Inspection • Operational Testing Summary • PQ1|

*PQ1 refers to the initial stage of PQ used to demonstrate that the water from the use points meets the specifications. It is completed before the system is used for production.

# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

Other documentation required to support acceptance and release (e.g., drawings and procedures) is managed through other processes (see Chapter 8).

# 7.1.3 Quality Unit Oversight

To ensure that the deliverables meet quality expectations, it is good practice to ensure engagement of the Quality Unit as part of an integrated team during the generation of test documentation that will be used to support qualification. The organization/site needs to determine how Quality Unit oversight will be provided for the testing and associated documentation of the CAs/CDEs. There are several appropriate methods, some of which are described below:

- Procedures that define GEP and the approach that will be used can be approved by the Quality Unit
- Standard test procedures (test scripts) and the associated results templates can be pre-approved by the Quality Unit
- Test strategy and acceptance criteria can be defined in the C&Q Plan for the Quality Unit to approve; the Quality Unit can then review the results as part of the development of the C&Q summary report
- Testing used to demonstrate that the CAs/CDEs identified in the System Risk Assessment can be covered in an Installation and Operational Qualification (IOQ) protocol for Quality Unit pre and post-execution review and approval
- Inspection and test records can be marked up to show the tests that are used to demonstrate the CAs/CDEs identified in the System Risk Assessment, or these tests can be assembled in a subsection of the test document for Quality Unit pre and post-execution review and approval

# 7.1.4 Roles and Responsibilities

The planning, execution, and closeout of testing should be performed by a multidisciplinary team (which includes vendors, engineering, and users) with appropriate Quality Unit oversight. Refer to Section 6.4 (Example Approval Matrix) for an example of the roles and responsibilities for the review and approval of deliverables.

The following roles are typically included in a C&Q team:

- Testing coordinator
- Production user (system user)
- Technical experts (engineering, automation, validation, or other SME)
- Quality Unit representative Mr. Rodolfo Russo

The testing coordinator represents the project manager in this scope of work. In small projects it is possible that the testing coordinator and the system owner is the same person, whereas for large projects it may be reasonable to designate the role to another individual. The testing coordinator is responsible for the coordination of all testing and ensuring that all tests are completed to meet the schedule.

Production users represent the system owners who will operate the system. In large construction projects, this group may be the engineering representatives that designed the system, due to their knowledge of the planned operation and requirements. Production users are responsible for ensuring the system operates as expected and that documentation (such as operational procedures and batch records) are developed so that they are approved for acceptance and release.

For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
---
# ISPE Baseline® Guide: Commissioning and Qualification

Technical experts are part of or support the testing team, as appropriate. Where testing activities are performed by vendors or third parties, the responsibilities between both parties need to be defined and documented. The Quality Unit representative provides oversight to ensure that all CAs/CDEs identified in the System Risk Assessment are tested.

# 7.2 Prerequisites

This section describes the activities that should be completed prior to execution of C&Q testing.

# 7.2.1 Planning

When C&Q testing is executed as a planned, documented, and managed engineering approach to the start-up and turnover of systems, the result is a safe and functional environment that meets established requirements and stakeholder expectations in a cost-effective way. Where such systems are direct impact (as determined through system classification described in Chapter 3), qualification is required.

The optimum test strategy can minimize repeat testing and help to reduce time in project schedules. For example, Pre-Delivery Inspection (PDI) and FAT may be used to support on-site inspection if appropriate approvals are obtained prior to execution. Components such as instruments can be verified (for aspects such as, e.g., manufacturer, model number, materials of construction) at the vendor site during a PDI or FAT. These checks can be used to support qualification activities, and do not need to be repeated provided there is justification that the functionality will not be affected by any subsequent activities prior to acceptance and release.

See Chapter 6 for details regarding C&Q planning.

# 7.2.2 Training

Each individual taking a role in the scope of C&Q testing needs have the appropriate training and experience to be able to complete their tasks effectively. Additional training may be required for setup and operation of a specific system, safety, and documentation standards required for a project.

# 7.2.3 Design Review/Design Qualification

DR/DQ should be completed with approval of the design before purchasing or starting construction of the system. This approved design then provides the basis for any subsequent engineering changes.

Fast track projects may proceed with construction or assembly at risk. However, the basis for approving system design and initiating change management needs to be clearly defined and, for direct impact systems, approved by the Quality Unit.

See Chapter 5 for details regarding DR and DQ.

# 7.2.4 Use of Vendor Documentation and Testing

Vendor documentation, including protocols and/or test plans (e.g., FAT, SAT), can be used. The strategy should be captured in the C&Q Plan. It is acceptable for the vendor to use their own format for conducting and documenting the testing provided that the vendor company documentation standards are acceptable. The protocol or test plan should be reviewed and approved by the owner and vendor (and Quality Unit if necessary, see Section 7.1.3) before the testing starts.

See Chapter 10 for details regarding the use of vendor documentation.

For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.

# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

# 7.2.5 Changes

Before testing, it is recommended that the relevant System Risk Assessment and change management records are reviewed to ensure that the testing documents reflect the installed system and no changes have been made that may impact the testing.

See Chapter 12 for details regarding change management.

# 7.3 Execution

This section describes the testing and inspection activities. Each organization/project should dictate the testing and inspection required, when it should be performed, and the titles of the record documents.

Tests should be planned and performed so that the results remain valid throughout subsequent phases of the project. Where this is not possible, there should be an evaluation to identify necessary retesting that may be required.

It is not necessary to complete each stage before proceeding to the next (e.g., moving from installation testing to operational testing) as long as:

- Any outstanding activities and critical discrepancies are noted and tracked with scheduled completion dates.
- There is documented evidence that the outstanding activities and critical discrepancies will not impact the subsequent work. For direct impact systems, this justification should be approved by the Quality Unit.

See Chapter 8 for information on the review and closure of testing documentation.

# 7.3.1 Vendor and System Construction Phase Documents

# 7.3.1.1 Turnover Packages

The purpose of the Turnover Package (TOP) is to provide complete documentation on system components, equipment, and setup. This documentation should also include as-built documentation (agreed upon data, drawings, software, etc.) turned over to the end-user. The TOP may be used throughout the system lifecycle, including ongoing maintenance, system change management, and retirement.

TOPs should be reviewed to ensure that they are complete and represent the installed system. Once completed, the TOP should be accepted and approved. Updates to the TOP should be included in the change management of the system where appropriate. Where documentation from the TOP is used to support operations or represent the basis for qualification, they should be stored in a suitable location that provides adequate control and access.

# Contents of the TOP

The TOP contents are usually determined by factors such as organization/site procedures, end-user preferences, GEP, system type, and contracts with the vendors. Information included in a TOP should ensure controlled operation, maintenance, and change. A complex novel system would, therefore, require more types of information than a simple off-the-shelf pump. Due to the variability of systems, vendors, and documentation, the TOP contents need to be flexible.

A typical TOP may include the following lifecycle related documents:

- Vendor-provided SOPs
- Spare parts lists

# ISPE Baseline® Guide: Commissioning and Qualification

- Maintenance requirements
- Maintenance records for work conducted prior to turnover
- As-built drawings
- Material of construction certificates
- Construction test and inspection records (e.g., pressure tests)
- Loop calibration documentation
- Material safety data sheets
- System start-up and shutdown information
- Warranties
- Records of adjustable settings
- Any specific documentation required by local regulations

The TOP contents should be clearly set out and agreed upon by all parties prior to placement of orders. The delivery
of the documentation requirements should not present a risk to the project at a later stage, from either content or
timing aspects.

# TOP Formats

System vendors typically have their own format for TOPs, but the owner organization should clearly define a
standardized format that aligns with their document control processes. This can help to ensure consistency between
the various TOPs that are submitted. The owner organization can propose a standard format or accept the vendor
format if it meets internal requirements. The format might be system-based. The format of TOPs should ensure
that they are user friendly with tabulated sections. TOPs should be obtained for all operating systems. To facilitate
storage, electronic versions of these documents are often required by the owner organization.

# 7.3.1.2 Cleaning

Where appropriate, the specifications should include clean construction or post-construction cleaning requirements to
ensure that systems are ready to test and/or turn over to the owner organization. Clean construction cleaning requirements may include pre-installation checks/inspection, sealing all openings during
construction, etc. Post-construction cleaning may include system cleaning, flushing, sanitation, and passivation,
where applicable. The activities should be documented.

Cleaning of any systems that will be moved into a classified area should be performed to prevent contamination.

# 7.3.2 Installation Testing (Acceptance Testing, Commissioning, and Qualification)

The verification of installation provides documentation that the system (or system components) is installed correctly,
is ready to start-up, and meets the design requirements including those identified as CDEs. Testing may be conducted within a single record/plan or separated into FAT, SAT, commissioning, and qualification
phases.

# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

The scope of installation testing includes all system installation requirements within the system boundaries, as defined in the specifications or C&Q Plan. Installation testing should include all mechanical, electrical, and automation components, and verify that they are in a state that as-built drawings and punch lists may be established.

Mechanical aspects may include, but are not limited to:

- Connection to appropriate utilities, services, and IT infrastructure
- Installation (e.g., pressure testing, slope verification, valve drain verification)
- System walk down with results marked to record the current system status, redline drawings updated, and identified issues captured as discrepancies

Construction records may include, but are not limited to:

- Component details
- Room finishes, e.g., flooring, coving, doors, windows, ceilings, drains, panels, penetrations (wall, ceiling, floor), etc. are installed as specified
- Construction records, e.g., welding records, material verification, leak/pressure test records, passivation records, component certificates of conformity

Automation and electrical records may include, but are not limited to:

- Electrical drawing walk down, e.g., data communication, alarm systems connections, cable trays, and conduits are installed and labeled
- Electrical cabinet construction and installation, e.g., are of appropriate waterproof classification
- Software version and configuration settings identification and backup
- Control panel wiring diagrams
- Screen navigation testing
- Hard-wired alarm and interlock testing
- Power/utility loss and recovery testing
- Communication loss and recovery testing
- Loop testing, calibration, or tuning
- Data integrity and audit trail testing

# 7.3.2.1 Pre-Delivery Inspections

PDIs are construction checks (inspection and testing) of systems or major system components before delivery to the owner site. These are typically done to verify construction progress and quality standards and to ensure delivery as scheduled. PDIs include inspection of parts (e.g., baffles in a heating jacket) that would not be able to be inspected during FAT or where a FAT cannot be performed for the system. This allows for remedy of any failings and avoids the delays to schedule (and associated costs) that would result from discovering problems later on-site.

For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
---
# 7.3.2.2 Factory Acceptance Test: Installation Tests

The FAT is inspection/testing of the system at the vendor (system manufacturer) site. The objective of the FAT is to provide testing and documentation at the earliest possible time, to verify that the vendor has fulfilled contractual responsibilities and the system meets the requirements. This allows for quicker and more efficient remedy of any failings and avoids the delays to schedule that would result from discovering problems later, on-site.

After completing the FAT, the owner representative agrees with the vendor whether the system is ready to be shipped or on pre-conditions for shipping. Agreements are documented and approved by the vendor and owner.

Where a system needs to be disassembled for shipping, the process should be documented. Details should include how the system will be disassembled, any marking or labeling of components, and how it will be reassembled and tested on-site. This information provides the rationale for testing that can be accepted from the FAT as well as testing that will be repeated as part of the SAT.

# 7.3.2.3 Receipt Inspection

Receipt Inspection (RI) provides documentation that delivered components, materials, or systems conform to the requirements, e.g., manufacturer, model, serial number as applicable with the required supporting documentation. This is typically done immediately upon delivery of the component to the owner site.

RI also provides documentation that all components have been received in good condition and are tagged correctly, where applicable.

Any items that do not pass RI are separated for issue resolution. Items that pass RI are stored under suitable conditions for installation.

# 7.3.2.4 Site Acceptance Test: Installation Tests

The SAT is the testing of the system at the owner organization site. The objective of the SAT is to provide testing and documentation of the installed system, to verify that the vendor has fulfilled contractual responsibilities and that the system meets the requirements prior to contractually accepting the system.

The SAT test plan includes a review of the FAT punch list (items carried over from the FAT that did not meet the acceptance criteria/expected results) and the plan to resolve open items. The SAT also includes reconfirming FAT test results where required due to system disassembly and reassembly.

After completing SAT testing, the owner representative agrees with the vendor whether the system is ready to be turned over to the owner or on pre-conditions for system turnover. Agreements are documented and approved by the vendor and owner. Acceptance of a system with open discrepancies can be made with the approval of the Testing Coordinator and System Owner.

# 7.3.2.5 Installation Verification

Installation verification includes verifying quality requirements related to installation (CDEs as defined through the System Risk Assessment). Example activities include:

- Verification that the product, ingredient and/or cleaning material contacting parts meet the specifications (materials of construction and finishes, as applicable)
- Installation of critical instruments
- Calibration of critical instruments (calibration could also be verified at the start of the functional testing, prior to the commencement of testing)

# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

# 7.3.3 System Start-Up

System start-up is a GEP approach to ensure that the system is energized and started up in a safe manner. The system start-up includes all mechanical, electrical, automation, and HSE related system functionalities. This is also where adjustments to the system, settings to regulate the system, or any balancing needed are made. A prerequisite to system start-up is that the system is mechanically complete without open critical punch list items for safety. System start-up may be conducted both at the vendor facility and after installed on-site.

The vendor operation or start-up manual should be reviewed prior to energizing the system. For systems that include vendor-provided control systems, training should be completed prior to energizing the system. Training of system operators, engineers, and maintenance personnel should be conducted in association with system vendors and properly documented.

System start-up is a four-step process, consisting of:

1. Verification that the system is complete with all valve switches in the correct position. For stick-built systems and modular systems, ensure that the integrated system (including automation) has been reviewed before it is energized.
2. Pre-start-up safety review.
3. Supervised energizing of the system utilities.
4. Pre-functional check of the system, with focus on safety functions.

Note that Steps 1 and 2 above need to be completed before Step 3. Step 3 needs to be completed prior to Step 4.

Refer to Chapter 21 (Appendix 7) for the following examples:

- System Pre-Start-Up Checklist
- Energizing the System
- Pre-Functional Testing Checklist

Any issues identified during system start-up activities should be verified to not impact future testing and addressed during the following testing steps.

# 7.3.4 Operational/Functional Testing

Operational/functional testing verifies that the system functionality matches what has been approved in the specifications (including those identified as CAs) and is suitably documented in a test plan/record for the full desired range of system operations in the target environment.

The sequence of functionalities being tested should follow a planned approach. Functional tests should be executed after completion of all installation tests that can impact the functional tests. Testing begins after system start-up and calibrations defined in the respective plan have been completed.

Testing may be conducted within a single report/test plan or separated into FAT, SAT, commissioning, and qualification phases. Installation and operational testing may be combined in a single document.

For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
---
# ISPE Baseline® Guide: Commissioning and Qualification

The scope of functional testing includes verifying system functionality. Functional testing may include mechanical, electrical, automation, and HSE related aspects. Documents, drawings, and operational procedures may be drafted and redlined, and discrepancy lists may be established. Initial operational tests of mechanical systems and general operation of the system components is performed.

Typically, the final step of functional testing is a full operational testing. This can be a full load test of the system to encompass worst case conditions. This test may need to be deferred if it is not possible to provide the relevant conditions. Final tuning of control loops may need to be delayed until system balancing has been performed (e.g., air or water balancing) or until enough system loads are available to establish various load levels, including full load. Operational testing should demonstrate the ability of the system to perform its specified functions throughout the expected operational range (which may be different than the specified range for equipment design/build purposes), encompassing worst case conditions. Where systems are interconnected (e.g., packaging line), operational verification should confirm satisfactory operation of the integrated line.

Functional testing includes the following activities, for example:

- Testing integrated automation controls and equipment for the whole system developed from the knowledge of processes, systems and equipment for both normal and alarm/failure modes
- Testing analog control loops at a range of values representing the intended system use – the range of testing should encompass the anticipated range of normal operation, including upper and lower operating limits (i.e., worst case conditions) without inducing process failure
- Confirming the system can handle predictable simultaneous use scenarios for utility systems
- Determining and recording manual component settings (e.g., valve positions, limit switch/screw positions, control switch settings)

# 7.3.4.1 Factory Acceptance Test and Site Acceptance Test: Operational Tests

After installation testing and system start-up, the FAT and SAT test documents may include execution of operational testing.

# 7.3.4.2 Qualification Operational Tests

Operational tests that demonstrate the ability of the system to meet the CAs/CDE functionalities are considered part of the qualification of the system and require Quality Unit oversight. Complex or novel systems may require formal system integrated operational testing to document PQ/PPQ operational criteria/control strategy.

# 7.3.5 Performance Qualification Testing

Performance Qualification (PQ) testing is the documented verification that manufacturing systems (e.g., when they are connected to other manufacturing systems) perform effectively and reproducibly, based on the approved process or analytical method and product specification, applying either original product or representative placebo. Cycle development and/or engineering tests are often necessary prior to performing PQ testing.

Completion of installation and operational testing (as applicable) to ensure the systems are operating correctly is required before performing cycle development; PQ testing can then be conducted. Operational SOPs should be used during PQ.

For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
---
# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

Examples of systems where PQ is usually required before acceptance and release include:

- Autoclaves
- Filling machines
- Pharmaceutical water and steam systems

# 7.4 Discrepancy Management

This section describes how to handle C&Q test results that do not meet the expected results or acceptance criteria, also referred to in this Guide as discrepancies. Organizations may have different terminology for these occurrences. Discrepancies related to CAs/CDEs are critical and require Quality Unit approval prior to closure.

Expected results are engineering expectations; failure to meet these should be reported to the design authority/SME who can accept them with a suitable justification or initiate engineering changes to obtain a suitable result (see Chapter 12). The design authority has the legal obligation to ensure the system meets codes so that approval is essential. An example of an expected result is the system operating pressure of a chilled water system.

Acceptance criteria are those testing/inspection results that must be met for a system to meet the requirements, which includes quality requirements. Examples of acceptance criteria include:

- Flow rate in a chilled water system: Low flow rates indicate the system cooling capacity could be inhibited
- Loop return pressure in a WFI system: This is a quality requirement (CA) because a negative pressure means a potential risk of contaminating the loop if a valve is opened

Table 7.3 provides suggested actions depending on the type of discrepancy.

# ISPE Baseline® Guide: Commissioning and Qualification

# Table 7.3: Discrepancy Types and Suggested Actions

|Issue|Description|Suggested Action|
|---|---|---|
|Obvious typographical error|Protocol generation error|- Add notation to test documentation with supporting documentation as needed, following Good Documentation Practices (see Chapter 13).
|
|Expected result not met|Design authority/SME accepts the result or initiates engineering change to obtain suitable result|- Add rationale to test record sheet with approval by the design authority/SME.
- The design authority has the legal obligation to ensure the system meets codes.
|
|Expected result or acceptance criteria not met with known root cause|Discrepancy|- Mark test record sheet to reflect the issue and required adjustments with any required repeat test.
- If engineering change is needed to make the adjustment, add cross reference in test record sheet.
- The rationale/reference is signed off by an SME and tests repeated.
|
|Acceptance criteria not met|Critical discrepancy with Quality Unit involvement|- Mark test record sheet to reflect the issue.
- Use discrepancy form to document the investigation results and steps for adjustments. Complete and record the adjustments. The form (or other reference containing the rationale) is signed off by an SME and Quality Unit representative.
- Retest with a new test record sheet.
|
|Acceptance criteria that cannot be met|Critical discrepancy with Quality Unit involvement|- Mark test record sheet to reflect the issue.
- Use discrepancy form to document the investigation results, justification, and any additional risk controls implemented. The form is signed off by an SME and Quality Unit representative.
|

- Critical discrepancies relate to CAs/CDEs.

# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

# 8 Acceptance and Release

# 8.1 Introduction

This chapter provides guidance on the acceptance and release phase which is where all testing is formally reviewed and accepted. The conclusion of the acceptance and release phase is that the system can be released for manufacturing operations, the next stage of qualification (e.g., for a WFI system), or for use in the manufacture of PPQ or commercial batches.

Acceptance and release represents the initiation of the “qualified state” and the beginning of QA-managed Quality Change Control (see Chapter 12), continuous performance monitoring, and periodic review. The project team transitions system ownership to operations/engineering.

The acceptance and release phase should occur as a cooperative review between the appropriate SMEs, including a representative from the department that will accept care, custody, and control of the system. The SMEs should review the results from an overall technical perspective.

If the system includes CAs/CDEs (direct impact), then the Quality Unit needs to be part of the acceptance and release review and approval, focusing on CAs/CDEs. Acceptance of a system with open discrepancies should be a Quality Unit decision. Typically, all discrepancies have to be addressed; however, non-product impacting discrepancies may remain open with an appropriate approved action plan.

The acceptance and release phase is not intended to be a catchall for closeout of all system or project documentation. For a commissioned system, the engineering SME will make a determination as to the requirements prior to release. For a qualified system, the Quality Unit and an engineering/process SME should determine the minimum requirements.

# 8.2 Prerequisites for the Acceptance and Release Phase

The installation, operational, and performance (as required) testing activities that should be completed prior to the acceptance and release phase include:

- Testing results should be reviewed
- Unexpected results should be investigated
- Discrepancies to the testing plan should be addressed
- Engineering changes should be closed
- Discrepancies should be addressed through Engineering Change Management and/or adjusted and retested

# 8.3 Requirements to Complete the Acceptance and Release Phase

In order to complete the acceptance and release phase, the following items are required to be addressed: completion of test documentation, closure of discrepancies and changes, and implementation of quality system elements. This section of the chapter describes these items and establishes minimum requirements to include in reports. Options for organizing and documenting these items are presented in the next section (Section 8.4).

For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
---
# 8.3.1 Completion of Testing Documentation

All of the planned testing needs to be successfully executed and the documentation completed. Typically, a team of SMEs including the Quality Unit representative (for testing related to CAs/CDEs) reviews the documentation prior to release. At least one SME review should be documented within the executed tests.

For the purposes of acceptance and release, the documentation for completion of testing should be approved by the team of SMEs and include the following information at a minimum:

- Testing description (component verification, calibration verification, etc.)
- Testing expected results and if met or not met
- Testing acceptance criteria and if met or not met
- Reference to test protocol and execution data (e.g., vendor documents, commissioning)
- Summary and conclusion

The content and complexity of the summary report may vary with the method of demonstrating that the acceptance criteria were met. Examples include:

- A report that lists the items noted above with an indication of Pass or Fail. In this case, the supporting executed test data should be assembled into package(s) and presented with the report.
- A more detailed report for each of the items noted above and that includes data to demonstrate that the acceptance criteria were met. In this case, the report may be presented alone without requiring the executed tests.

# 8.3.2 Closure of Discrepancies

Discrepancies from the protocol acceptance criteria should be categorized by potential impact. Discrepancies that do not impact product quality may remain open. The report should contain an indication of any open discrepancies along with potential manufacturing planning implications. These discrepancies should be assigned to a responsible individual with defined responsibility for resolution within a specific timeline.

Discrepancies that could impact product quality need to be closed. All discrepancies impacting product quality should be recorded, addressed, retested where necessary, and successfully closed prior to completing the acceptance and release phase. All discrepancies should be listed with status (closed or open) within the summary report.

# 8.3.3 Closure of Engineering Changes

All changes should be handled in accordance with change management (as described in Chapter 12). The system changes that have occurred during the testing phase should be closed as part of the acceptance and release phase, unless there is agreement with the Quality Unit for these open changes to be carried forward. These changes should be assigned to a responsible individual with defined responsibility for resolution within a specific timeline.

All engineering changes should be listed with status (closed or open) within the summary report.

# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

# 8.3.4 Implementation of Quality System Elements

For systems that contain CAs/CDEs, the implementation of quality systems is required prior to releasing a system for manufacturing operations; this ensures that the system can operate and be maintained consistently and remains in a qualified state. These aspects are referred to as quality system elements and may include (as applicable), but are not limited to:

- Availability of SOPs for operation of the system
- Calibration/maintenance system and associated procedures
- Mechanism for tracking system use and maintenance (e.g., logbook)
- Vendor documentation (e.g., maintenance requirements, spare parts)
- Operator training within the training quality system
- Completion of any other organization requirements to add the system into the operating facility

A statement that all required quality system elements are in place should be included in the summary report for systems with CAs/CDEs. If a system is released with outstanding items (this requires Quality Unit approval), a quality system should be in place to track these items to closure with an appropriate level of Quality Unit oversight.

# 8.4 Summary Report Organization

The acceptance and release process is completed through a summary report. The contents and complexity of the report may vary, but it needs to include SME review and approval of the following items:

- Closure of testing documentation
- Discrepancies
- Engineering changes
- Implementation of quality system elements (as required)
- Documented formal release of the system

The summary report can be accomplished in several different ways depending on the scale and complexity of the project. A separate document for the summary report is not always necessary. This section describes example options for the summary report.

# 8.4.1 Example Option #1: Commissioning Summary Report – Systems with No Critical Aspects/Critical Design Elements

- Completion of testing documentation
- Closure of punch list items and discrepancies
- Closure of engineering changes

# 8.4.3 Example Option #3: Formal Release of System with Use of Change Control – Existing Facility

When new systems or changes to existing systems are implemented through QA-managed Quality Change Control (as described in Chapter 12), some or all of the requirements (as described in Section 8.3) may be contained within the change control.

For existing facilities, it is common to verify the implementation of quality system elements and document the formal release of the system in the change control rather than in the Qualification Summary Report. In this case, the Qualification Summary Report should include a reference to the change control record. The use of the change control allows for closure of the testing phase in a timely manner.

# 8.4.4 Example Option #4: Acceptance and Release Report – Large Projects with Interconnected Systems

For large projects with interconnected systems, multiple reports may be used to complete the acceptance and release phase. In this case, the completion of testing for each system may be documented with separate summary reports. An overall acceptance and release report would reference the completed summary reports for each of the interconnected systems (rather than listing the individual test descriptions, acceptance criteria, and results); this report would document completion of all quality system elements and include the formal release statement. The benefit to this process is that it allows the testing to be closed out prior to finalizing procedures which often include information from performance testing or testing of multiple linked systems.

With the use of a separate acceptance and release report, it is possible to release a grouping of systems. One strategy, as shown in Figure 8.4, may be to release per processing area (e.g., cell culture) after all testing is completed and closed out with separate summary reports. A separate acceptance and release report would be used to capture the completion of testing documentation and quality system elements implementation for the entire processing area or grouping of systems.

Note: There are several factors to consider when using this approach. The grouping strategy may complicate future changes. The grouping would also require presenting information from all systems during regulatory audits even when only a single system is requested. The advantages are that qualification would more closely reflect the grouping created by the process risk assessment as well as the batch record structure and eliminate potential gaps with the integration of systems.

# Figure 8.4: Example Option #4: Documents with Use of Acceptance and Release Report

|Completion of Testing Documentation|Completion of Testing Documentation|Completion of Testing Documentation|
|---|
|Bioreactor 101|Qualification Summary Report|Bioreactor 201|
|Cell Culture|Media Hold Test|Qualification Summary Report|
|SIP Test Summary|Cell Culture Acceptance and Release Report|Released for Manufacturing or PPQ|
|Media Feed Tanks Qualification Summary Report|Cleaning Test Summary Report| |
|Mr. Rodolfo Russo|RHO, MILANO|Quality System Qualification ID number: 364201|
|Summary Report|Elements in Place Closure of Engineering Changes|Statement of Release|

# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

# 8.5 Acceptance and Release Completion

Approval and release is completed when the Quality Unit approves and signs the summary report, which could be the Qualification Summary Report, change control closure, or Acceptance and Release Summary Report. This final approval releases the system for use. Refer to Section 6.4 for an example document approval matrix.

Quality Unit acceptance and release represents the initiation of the “qualified state” and the beginning of QA-managed Quality Change Control (described in Chapter 12), continuous performance monitoring, and periodic review.

# 8.6 Traceability Matrix

A Traceability Matrix may be used as a tool to identify CAs/CDEs, acceptance criteria, and includes reference to the testing document (planned or completed). Best practice is to create the matrix early in the project or as soon as the CAs/CDEs are identified. The Traceability Matrix can be used throughout the project as an engineering tool to manage DR, testing activities, results, and documentation that ensures all CAs/CDEs have been met. It provides traceability for each URS requirement by design and through testing.

As such, the Traceability Matrix is not required to be approved. Approvals are completed for the test protocols and the Qualification Summary Report or Acceptance and Release Report to demonstrate completion of all testing. The Traceability Matrix is not a required standalone deliverable.

For smaller projects, a Traceability Matrix may not be needed. It is considered a useful project management tool when testing is planned to be completed in several different documents or stages conducted by different companies and there may not be any other format to organizing the documents. It is recommended to keep the amount of information managed in this document to a minimum to avoid an unmanageable document size when printed and to simplify tracking of changes.

# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

# 9 Periodic Review

# 9.1 Introduction

There is a regulatory requirement to maintain qualified systems in a validated state, i.e., the qualification documents remain valid and the system remains suitable for the intended purpose (refer to Chapter 15 for the regulatory basis). Quality systems are in place to maintain that status and should be subject to internal audit to ensure robustness. These quality systems are:

- Change control
- Maintenance
- Calibration
- Deviation

It is regulatory expectation that there is also a periodic review process to ensure that systems can be considered qualified (refer to Chapter 15 for the regulatory basis). This chapter describes an approach for periodic review where the frequency of review or requalification is established based on risk, the current level of process understanding and process performance, and regulatory requirements.

# 9.1.1 Rationale

Certain systems have regulatory expectations to confirm ongoing system performance through periodic verification; such systems include sterilizing autoclaves, depyrogenation tunnels/ovens, and equipment with a Sterilize-in-Place (SIP) functionality (e.g., freeze dryer). Table 9.1 summarizes examples of periodic verification frequencies for systems with regulatory expectations.

This Document is licensed to Mr. Rodolfo Russo RHO, MILANO ID number: 364201 Downloaded on: 7/18/20 2:03 AM For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
---
# ISPE Baseline® Guide: Commissioning and Qualification

# Table 9.1: Examples of Periodic Verification Frequencies for Systems with Regulatory Expectations

|System|Periodic Verification Scope|Frequency|Regulatory Basis|
|---|---|---|---|
|Facilities|Room classification|No period defined¹|N/A|
|HEPA filter leak testing|ISO 5/Grade A and ISO 7/Grade B: Semi-annual²| |[23]|
|ISO 5/Grade A cabinets used for GMP applications (e.g., biological safety cabinet, laminar flow hoods, isolators, downflow hoods)| |Semi-annual|[23]|
|Steam Sterilizers|All sterilization cycles|Annual|[24]|
|Equipment SIP|SIP cycle|Annual³|[24]|
|Depyrogenation Tunnel|Depyrogenation cycle|Annual|[24]|
|Aseptic Filling Lines|Aseptic process simulation|Semi-annual|[23]|

General Note: Local requirements should be verified.

# Notes:

1. For additional information regarding area classification, refer to the ISPE Baseline® Guide: Sterile Product Manufacturing Facilities [25] which takes the following into account: ISO 14644-1 Classification of Air Cleanliness [26], the FDA September 2004 Guidance for Industry Sterile Drug Products Produced by Aseptic Processing – Current Good Manufacturing Practice [23], and Annex 1 of the EU GMPs [24].
2. Industry practice for ISO 8/Grade C and Grade C is annual.
3. If the SIP process is continuously monitored, it is usually not revalidated.

For other types of systems, organizations have traditionally used an annual detailed review of the change history, maintenance, and operational records to provide this confirmation. However, this approach has typically provided a small return when compared to the considerable investment to conduct the review process. In addition, there is concern that, due to the scale of the effort involved, there is a significant risk that important performance signals may be missed.

This Guide proposes applying a science and risk-based approach to periodic review:

- For a critical utilities system (such as HVAC, clean compressed air, pharmaceutical water and steam systems) routine monitoring includes:
- - Regular reviews of the results
- Regular reporting of system performance trends, preventative maintenance and calibration metrics
- Regular check of qualification drawings (typically biannually) and of system tagging (typically annually)

If routine monitoring is performed, then no additional review is considered necessary. The monitoring confirms system performance and the drawing verification confirms that documentation is up-to-date.
- For other systems (e.g., manufacturing systems), the relative potential risk to product quality for different system types is evaluated and the frequency of review is defined based on the risk.

For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
---
# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

This proposed approach is a triage process that looks for any unusual performance signals at a high level. If none are observed, then the review is complete. If there are signals observed, then a deeper review is conducted that may result in detailed investigation and testing.

# 9.1.2 Principles/Concepts

This periodic review approach is most efficient if the organization/site has mature quality systems and the associated quality system performance metrics. These metrics could include:

- Monitoring of maintenance to ensure scheduled activities are not delayed beyond the permitted calendar window
- Monitoring of breakdown/unplanned maintenance activities for frequency and recurrence of specific issues
- Monitoring of calibration to ensure any scheduled checks/activities are not delayed beyond the permitted calendar window
- Robust change control system with categorization of changes
- Robust deviation system with categorization of deviations
- Robust review of computer system audit trails and security at procedurally controlled intervals including:
- Confirming users are not using generic names or shared accounts
- Ensuring that there are no redundant users or obsolete users, e.g. for staff who have changed roles or left the organization (i.e., good user access management)
- Reviewing the audit trail of administrator actions, ensuring a quality system record justifies each action, and listing the audit trails reviewed
- Reviewing the audit trail of other user category actions, ensuring a quality system record justifies the actions (if applicable), and listing the audit trails reviewed
- Reviewing the audit trail for changes to critical configuration settings identified in the data integrity assessment, ensuring a quality system record justifies the changes, and listing the audit trails reviewed
- Verifying that audit trails reviews were performed as dictated in the system administrator SOP and operational SOP
- Making sure the Quality Unit reviews the audit trail as verifier
- Verifying backup and archiving of GMP critical data

The use of these performance metrics can minimize the amount of effort required to obtain the information for the periodic review. Where these mature quality systems are not in place, the same principles can be used, but the process may not be as efficient.

Figure 9.1 provides an overview of the periodic review process.

# 9.2 Periodic Review Approach

The periodic review approach proposed in this Guide consists of two phases:

- Phase 1: System Categorization and Schedule Assignment
- Phase 2: Periodic Review Execution

This Document is licensed to Mr. Rodolfo Russo, RHO, MILANO

ID number: 364201

It is strongly recommended that the period review process be led by an individual who has a broad range of experience. The Tier B and Tier C reviews should be conducted by a small team where necessary. This small team should consist of individuals who are knowledgeable in the system operating, maintenance, and calibration history; these are typically the system owner, maintenance, and a Quality Unit representative. The review should be approved, at a minimum, by the system owner and the Quality Unit. This approach is intended to provide an efficient process, where the overall review is accomplished in a timely manner.

For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
---
# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

# 9.2.1 Prerequisites

The required prerequisites for the periodic review process include:

- List of validated manufacturing systems
- Copy of relevant current regulations (i.e., those for the markets the facility supplies)
- For each system:
- As-built or as-is record drawing (qualification record drawing)
- Change system metrics or system change history
- Maintenance and calibration system metrics or system maintenance and calibration history
- Deviation system metrics or deviations related to the system performance (i.e., where a root cause investigation has highlighted equipment performance as a root cause)
- System validation/qualification documents
- Risk assessment (if available)

# 9.3 Phase 1: System Categorization and Schedule Assignment

The systems are categorized based on the potential to impact product quality. Typically, due to in-process testing, systems used for early stages of manufacture are considered lower risk than those used for stages closer to the finished product.

The category defines the periodic review frequency schedule. These frequencies should be based on SME determination, considering the potential for the system to impact product quality. Systems that have requirements defined in the relevant local regulations and/or regulatory guidance must be managed to meet those requirements.

Table 9.2 provides examples of periodic review categorization for various systems with the supporting rationale. Table 9.3 provides the associated frequencies for the periodic review categories.

# Table 9.2: Examples of Periodic Review Categories

|---|---|---|---|

- Decontamination/sterilization system (including equipment sterilization)
|- Complex systems with a quality critical function subject to regulatory guidance or Standard Operating Procedures
- Routine revalidation is already established
|
|Sterile Filtration*|0|- Filter/filter housing
- Filtration system
|Standard systems with pre- and post-use integrity tests used to monitor system performance|

|Fill/Finish|2|• Buffer preparation/hold systems • Capper • Filler|• Systems are generally constructed from standard components • Quality of the output is routinely monitored|
|Utilities and HVAC|0|• Compressed air system • Argon gas system • Carbon dioxide gas system • Helium gas system • Oxygen system • Nitrogen system • Anhydrous ammonia system • Urea system • Specialty gas system • Specialty chemical system • Ethanol system • Vacuum system • Clean steam system • Deionized water system • Purified water system • WFI system • HVAC system|• Systems are generally constructed from standard components • Quality of the output is routinely monitored|
|Packaging|0|• Packaging systems|• Systems are generally constructed from standard components • Quality of the output is routinely monitored|

*Where filters are used for quality critical applications, pre- and post-use integrity testing is used to confirm system performance; therefore, periodic review would not be not required.

# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

# Table 9.3: Example Periodic Review Schedule

|Periodic Review Category|Periodic Review Schedule|
|---|---|
|0|This category relies on existing quality systems and performance monitoring programs; periodic review is not required, e.g., critical utility systems such as compressed air, purified water or WFI.|
|1|This category has established requirements from regulations specific to the system and is not subject to additional periodic assessment activities, e.g., autoclaves and depyrogenation tunnels.|
|2|Perform reviews at two-year intervals.|
|3|Perform reviews at three-year intervals.|

# 9.4 Phase 2: Periodic Review Execution

Using the frequency schedules established in Phase 1 (System Categorization and Schedule Assignment), the periodic reviews can be performed using a three-tier approach which involves reviewing the following four quality systems:

- GMP compliance expectations
- Change control system
- Maintenance/calibration system
- Deviation system

The three-tier approach is summarized as follows:

- An initial assessment (Tier A) is done at a high level. If this initial assessment shows any signs that the system is not operating consistently, then the assessment progresses to the next level; if not, then the review is considered complete.
- The second level assessment (Tier B) is more detailed. If this assessment shows that there are any issues, then the assessment progresses to the next level. If this assessment concludes that the system is still performing satisfactorily, then the review is complete.
- The third level assessment (Tier C) involves defining and completing the actions required to determine the system to be suitable for the intended purpose, with the necessary supporting documentation updated as necessary.

The assessment period is the time period since the previous periodic review or since the original qualification.

Refer to Chapter 25 (Appendix 11) for an example periodic review form. Refer to Chapter 26 (Appendix 12) for information regarding periodic review of Controlled Temperature Chambers (CTCs), including an approach designed to address the system-specific issues.

Note: When a periodic review program is initially introduced, it is typical to cover the systems in each schedule class within the associated timeframe. For example, for systems with a two-year review period, the initial review of all systems in that schedule class can be completed in the first two years.

For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
