# ISPE Baseline® Guide: Commissioning and Qualification

# 9.4.1 Initial Assessment (Tier A)

To perform the initial assessment, the records from the four quality systems are reviewed to identify any concerns related to the qualified state of the system being assessed.

# GMP Compliance Expectations (Tier A)

If there is an organization/site quality system that monitors changes in regulatory requirements/expectations and proactively addresses changes within the organization/site, then this category does not need to be reviewed as part of periodic review.

If this system is not in place, then the Quality Unit should:

- Review changes in the relevant regulatory regulations and guidances for the assessment period, with the current scope of qualification reviewed to ensure it meets any changes in the requirements where necessary.
- Review related audit observations and determine if there are any changes in regulatory expectations.

# Change Control System (Tier A)

For the system being assessed:

- Perform a query in the change control system database for the assessment period.
- Review the query results and determine if there have been any reportable change requests with qualification impact that can affect the system quality attributes.

Note: Change records that have been determined to be low risk, and thus are not reportable to regulatory agencies, do not need to be reviewed as part of the periodic review process.

# Maintenance/Calibration System (Tier A)

For the system being assessed:

- Perform a query in the maintenance management system for the assessment period.
- Determine if there were repeated corrective maintenance and/or calibration records.

# Deviation System (Tier A)

For the system being assessed:

- Perform a query of the deviations for the assessment period.
- Review the query results for deviations with potential product quality impact where the root cause has been attributed to the system being assessed.

Note: Deviations that have already been determined to have little or no risk to product quality do not need to be reviewed as part of the periodic review process.

# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

After this initial assessment of each quality system:

- If any concerns (regarding the potential impact to the qualified state) within a quality system are identified, then a second level assessment needs to be performed. Note that the scope of the second level assessment is limited to the quality system(s) of concern.
- If no concerns are identified, then the assessment for that quality system is complete.

# 9.4.2 Second Level Assessment (Tier B)

The second level assessment should only be performed on each quality system category that was determined during the initial assessment to require further assessment. All second level assessment activities should be performed by representatives (SMEs) from the same departments as the initial assessment team.

# GMP Compliance Expectations (Tier B)

Using the information from the initial assessment:

- Evaluate the impact of the regulatory changes identified in the initial assessment
- In the judgment of the assessment team, determine if the changes present concerns about the qualified state of the system

# Change Control System (Tier B)

Using the information from the initial assessment:

- Evaluate the changes identified in the initial assessment
- In the judgment of the assessment team, determine if the number and/or type of changes identified present concerns about the qualified state of the system

# Maintenance/Calibration System (Tier B)

Using the information from the initial assessment:

- Review the maintenance and calibration records identified in the initial assessment (including corrective maintenance)
- In the judgment of the assessment team, determine if the records present concerns about the qualified state of the system

# Deviation System (Tier B)

Using the information from the initial assessment:

- Review the deviations with potential product impact identified in the initial assessment
- In the judgment of the assessment team, determine if the deviations indicate that the system performance is not consistent and thus may not be performing in a qualified state

# ISPE Baseline® Guide: Commissioning and Qualification

After this second level assessment of applicable quality system(s):

- If there are concerns that the system may no longer be in a qualified state, then a third level assessment needs to be performed to determine the actions required to address the concerns. Note that the scope of the third level assessment is limited to the quality system(s) of concern.
- If there is confidence that the qualified state of the system has not been impacted, then the assessment for that quality system is complete.

# 9.4.3 Third Level Assessment (Tier C)

The third level assessment should only be performed on each quality system category that was determined during the second level assessment to require further assessment. All third level assessment activities should be performed by representatives (SMEs) from the departments who have conducted the initial and second level assessments.

The assessment team should review the information from the second level assessment and establish the action(s) required to restore the system to a qualified state. A non-conformance record should be generated to manage the process.

# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

# 10 Vendor Assessment for C&Q

# Documentation Purposes

# 10.1 Introduction

This chapter describes vendor assessments3 and controls for work where vendors follow their own procedures to perform testing and produce documentation that may be used to support system qualification. When vendor tests are used to directly support qualification where appropriate, the result is reduced on-site testing and reduced cost and time in the schedule without impacting quality. In addition, the SMEs on the project team can contribute to improved product quality by focusing their attention on other higher risk quality critical activities.

Vendor assessments (for C&Q documentation purposes) do not include the vendor selection and/or commercial assessment, though the results may impact those assessments. Vendor assessments for C&Q documentation purposes may be integrated into this broader vendor selection and qualification process or comprise a standalone activity for C&Q planning and execution purposes.

Regulatory expectations for vendor assessments are provided in Chapter 15 (Appendix 1). For detailed information on assessing vendors of automated systems, refer to ISPE GAMP® 5: A Risk-Based Approach to Compliant GxP Computerized Systems [6]. For detailed information on vendor assessments for design services, construction services, and equipment supply, refer to ISPE Good Practice Guide: Project Management [27].

Note: It is not necessary to perform vendor assessments, as described in this Guide, for vendors that provide qualification services (sometimes referred to as secondary services) when those vendors are contracted to follow the regulated owner organization procedures and practices or applicable specialist standards, such as the US National Environmental Balancing Bureau (NEBB) [28].

# 10.1.1 Purpose and Benefits

The objective of a vendor assessment is to establish confidence in the vendor and the vendor quality systems to perform testing, manage changes, and provide documentation that is accurate, complete, and unambiguous, with results matching what was expected. Vendor verification documentation assessments are highly recommended when documents may be used to support qualification. Purchaser and vendor cooperation is key to successfully achieving this goal.

Vendor assessments can minimize business/project risk associated with vendor performance, including project cost and schedule risk, by performing testing and completing documentation deliverables required to support verification of CAs (qualification) right the first time.

The use of vendor documentation can prevent repeating work that was appropriately performed and documented and can help to achieve GMP compliance at a reduced cost and in less time. Vendors are the experts in their particular systems and the use of vendor testing and documentation should be encouraged. As an example, consider verification of product contact materials. Accepting a certificate of conformity from a quality vendor can be an acceptable, low-risk approach for material verification; checking 100% of the related heat certificates would therefore be unnecessary.

Downloaded on: 7/18/20 2:03 AM

3 The use of the term vendor assessment throughout this chapter relates specifically to the use of vendor-provided documentation to support system C&Q.

For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
---
# ISPE Baseline® Guide: Commissioning and Qualification

# 10.1.2 Principles/Concepts

It is good engineering practice to assess vendor’s capability for testing and documenting the systems they provide for
conformance to requirements and specifications, based on the criticality of the system. The decision and justification
to use vendor documentation to support the verification of CAs and associated CDEs should be based on the
intended purpose of the manufacturing system and the suitability of the vendor quality systems. The decision and
rationale should be documented in the C&Q Plan and approved by SMEs and the Quality Unit.

The Quality Unit and Engineering/Technical SME should agree on the type (e.g., checklist, test plan, execution
report) and quality (Good Documentation Practices) of the deliverables. The Quality Unit should review the
vendor assessment and confirm the extent to which the vendor’s documentation package may be used to support
qualification and verify suitability for the intended purpose. This review and confirmation should be conducted and
documented as early as possible.

Quality Unit oversight in the use of vendor verification documentation is a risk-based decision, with consideration of
risk to product quality. For example, there is less product quality risk in documenting pipe boroscoping or passivation
activities as compared to an SAT for a sterile filling isolator.

# 10.2 Communication of Requirements, Scope, and Deliverables

The project team should ensure that the fundamental project requirements are communicated effectively to all parties,
both internal and external. In addition to the overall project requirements, specific items that should be communicated
to vendors involved in the project should include, at a minimum:

- Specific scope of work, including requirements and specifications as appropriate
- Specific deliverables, including testing and documentation, that are in the scope of work
- Quality assurance requirements for the work and deliverables
- Project controls which will apply
- Schedule of deliverables, including documentation deliverables and lead times for review and update

Depending on the size of the project, these items may be contained in a single document or detailed in several
project plans, procedures, or specifications. Confirmation should be obtained that this communication has been
understood; provisions should be made in the schedule for dialogue and/or meetings to provide clarification and
confirm comprehension by the vendor.

From a project risk perspective and to help to ensure that quality is built in at the outset, arrangements should be
made for supervision, inspections, and/or surveillance for the scope of work and associated deliverables across the
lifecycle of their development.

# 10.3 Vendor Assessment Factors

As part of the vendor assessment for verification documentation purposes, the owner organization should assess
the robustness of the vendor’s Quality Management System (QMS). This provides insight into the strengths and
weaknesses of the vendor capability and provides an opportunity to align or, if necessary, impose procedures or
controls.

When performing a vendor assessment, the following factors should be evaluated:

- Degree of novelty of the system provided by the vendor

For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
---
# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

- Training and experience of the staff who will conduct the testing
- Facilities and equipment available for the testing
- Instrument calibration program
- Vendor quality oversight
- Engineering quality system
- Technical capabilities
- Procedures for material control, material processing, and incoming goods inspection
- Documentation standards (Good Documentation Practice)
- Software development and testing standards (testing, procedures)
- Fabrication facilities

Consideration should also be given to assessing the supply chain and whether a vendor has suitable procedures in place for assessing their own vendors (e.g., for welding, piping, automation hardware and software) and subcontractors.

As discussed in Section 10.1, a vendor assessment for verification documentation purposes is not a vendor qualification/audit. It is different than a product-focused vendor assessment for raw materials, packaging components, Active Pharmaceutical Ingredients (APIs), etc.

Refer to Chapter 27 (Appendix 13) for an example vendor assessment for verification documentation.

# 10.4 Vendor Assessment Strategy

A risk-based approach should be taken in determining the project-specific vendor assessment strategy. There are several approaches for completing vendor assessments; examples include:

- Use of existing vendor assessment from within the owner organization
- On-site self-assessment by the vendor with a standardized vendor assessment template or questionnaire provided by the owner organization
- On-site assessment by a technical SME or assessment team from the owner organization
- Waiver of assessment

The waiver of any project-generated formal assessment may be granted based on a vendor’s current qualification status, use history, previous audit, lack of criticality of vendor supplied information, or other documented and Quality Unit approved justification. The decision to reassess a vendor that has already been assessed should be based on risk, using the same factors that triggered the initial assessment.

The decisions and rationale for the approach taken can be documented and approved through the mechanism of the C&Q Plan or as a standalone activity. This may be part of an owner organization’s PQS (Pharmaceutical Quality System) procedures and routine practices.

For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
---
# 10.5 Use of Vendors Unfamiliar with GMP

The use of vendor assessments, quality assurance plans, and/or vendor quality plans provide a means either to accept or reject a vendor, or to identify where additional resources should be applied to ensure success. Certain vendors may not operate within a GMP environment for most of their products and services to customers. A QMS may be an unfamiliar or informal practice. The vendor needs to be informed that they are supplying goods or services to a pharmaceutical manufacturer.

Deficiencies should be recognized early and appropriate support measures established to help to deliver the necessary requirements. Where deficiencies are identified, additional controls and resources should be applied as early as possible. The vendor needs to support this approach and their agreement should be obtained. Additional control measures, designed to mitigate the risks presented, may include:

- Vendor training and guidance
- Additional review and approval
- Additional auditing of the vendor
- Attending/witnessing critical phases of the design, fabrication, and testing at the vendor site
- Support by third-party service providers
- Additional owner testing to compensate for being unable to rely on vendor generated test documentation

# 10.6 Vendor Training

In addition to the communication of project and quality requirements, training may be needed for vendor personnel. Such training requirements should be identified during the vendor assessment. Key areas of training include:

- Application of the owner organization’s PQS requirements in the daily work of vendor personnel
- Training on GMP (as applicable)
- Importance and maintenance of training records
- Acceptable documentation practices and procedures, including document version control
- Engineering Change Management or project procedures (for mechanical and automation, including software)
- Deviation management procedures
- Owner organization’s site specific documentation requirements (for on-site vendor personnel), including confidentiality
- Specialist task training

Appropriate training for team members taking over special roles (such as facilitation of risk assessments, SME reviews, tank entry, etc.) should be carefully planned and scheduled to ensure adequate resources that these specialist skills are available to meet project schedules.

For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.

# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

# 11 Engineering Quality Process

# 11.1 Introduction

When the C&Q process is considered as a part of an organization-wide PQS, the result is an achievement of both GMP and GEP concepts. A separate quality system is not required for C&Q; instead, it can be part of the overall PQS or a subset of the PQS. For the purposes of this Guide, the term Engineering Quality Process is used to describe the process within the PQS that covers C&Q. This Guide recognizes that this function is referred to differently across organizations which have their own structure and assignment of roles and responsibilities. This chapter intends to focus on the concepts that should be addressed for this function.

The overall organization PQS program provides the quality practices for processes in order to consistently deliver quality products. The Engineering Quality Process, as a subset process of the PQS, addresses the quality practices for the C&Q process and is intended to support the overall PQS program.

Both the PQS and Engineering Quality Process need to emphasize quality (i.e., the product or service meeting the specified requirements) versus compliance (i.e., meeting current procedures or organization requirements). Good quality practices and methods through the PQS produce robust reliable systems and ensures compliance. The Engineering Quality Process needs to be established as part of the PQS; the Engineering Quality Process can then be used for developing sustainable, consistent, cost-effective, and practical engineering processes for project and operational lifecycle management.

# 11.1.1 Rationale

Current regulatory authority and ICH [12] guidances emphasize a focus on building quality into the product⁴ and not by inspection. ICH Q10 [9] augments the defined GMPs by describing specific quality system elements and management responsibilities that should be in place to achieve three main objectives; it states:

“Implementation of the Q10 model should result in achievement of three main objectives which complement or enhance regional GMP requirements:
- Achieve Product Realisation [Section 1.5.1]
- Establish and Maintain a State of Control [Section 1.5.2]
- Facilitate Continual Improvement [Section 1.5.3]

”

The FDA 2006 Guidance for Industry: Quality Systems Approach to Pharmaceutical CGMP Regulations [15] states as the goal of the guidance:

“This guidance describes a comprehensive quality systems model, which, if implemented, will allow manufacturers to support and sustain robust, modern quality systems that are consistent with CGMP [Current Good Manufacturing Practice] regulations.”

In alignment with ICH Q8 [4], the FDA guidance states:

“The overarching philosophy articulated in both the CGMP regulations and in robust modern quality systems is: Quality should be built into the product, and testing alone cannot be relied on to ensure product quality.”

4 The term “building quality into processes” was first coined in June 1960 by Eiji Toyoda.

For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
---
# ISPE Baseline® Guide: Commissioning and Qualification

Another useful resource in understanding Engineering Quality Process as a philosophy is the work of Dr. W. Edwards Deming [29]. Deming believed in improving the process and in doing so using process measures to guide improvement efforts. In his book [29], Deming states:

“Conformance to specifications may be achieved in several ways:

1. By careful inspection, sorting the bad from the good. Dependence on inspection is hazardous and costly.
2. By work on the production process to shrink variation about the nominal value.”

The approach presented in this Guide embraces regulatory guidance and Deming’s principle that demonstration of conformance to specifications can be achieved by developing a process, which addresses causes and concerns for variability and reduces reliance on inspection. Thus, the focus of the Quality Unit should be to ensure adherence to a process that identifies CAs/ CDEs and confirms that they have been satisfactorily tested.

# 11.2 Application

The Engineering Quality Process should include aspects that support the C&Q effort, such as maintenance and calibration, change management program, critical utilities, and the procedures for the C&Q process. The approach described in this section is based on the six-system inspection model described in the FDA 2006 Guidance [15], whereby an organization’s overall quality system provides the foundation for the five manufacturing systems that function within it. The six systems are as follows:

- Quality system
- Production system
- Facilities and equipment system
- Laboratory control system
- Materials system
- Packaging and labeling system

Product and process development/knowledge may be added to this list or may be included as part of the production system. The concept is addressed in Chapters 4 and 6.

As part of the overall PQS, the Engineering Quality Process plays a significant role in providing the practices and controls within the facilities and equipment system.

The application of an Engineering Quality Process also supports other requirements, such as for HSE and efficiency. Understanding this broader context and embracing Engineering Quality Process as a philosophy, rather than simply a collection of tools and templates, supports the design of a sound C&Q program that delivers systems suitable for the intended purpose using cost-effective solutions.

An Engineering Quality Process can help to transform the way in which organizations currently operate and to reinforce the efficient implementation of a science and risk-based approach to facility start-up. The Engineering Quality Process has additional objectives, including to:

- Provide a lean framework to foster innovation, encourage (rather than obstruct) continual improvement, and facilitate the effective, cost-efficient, and focused application of time and resources

For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
---
# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

- Provide the framework for linking the CAs to the CDEs and the mechanism to capture the traceability
- Enable science and risk-based decisions for C&Q testing, based on internally and externally generated knowledge rather than just completion of generic forms
- Optimize lifecycle activities through smart management and maintenance of process and engineering knowledge
- Acknowledge the importance of personnel development by facilitating an SME-led environment which accredits or trains those individuals that are responsible for executing these practices

To establish an Engineering Quality Process, there needs to be a means for:

- Implementing the Engineering Quality Process by senior management, which requires engineering and Quality Unit sponsorship
- Implementing a proposed C&Q process (transforming current practice to a desired practice) with stakeholders engaged
- Identifying stakeholders
- Defining roles and responsibilities
- Identifying where the organization or site is in terms of implementation of the new C&Q process and the pathway for implementation
- Establishing what the stakeholders can contribute to the proposed process
- Measuring and maintaining progress, suitability, and effectiveness
- Informing and getting input from stakeholders on the proposed C&Q process

When properly applied, the Engineering Quality Process achieves the following:

- Delivers systems that are suitable for the intended purpose
- Provides a systematic engineering structure to define, develop, and implement efficient streamlined engineering processes
- Enables the assurance of quality and compliance with corporate policies, external regulations, and industry standards
- Reflects the scope and extent of the C&Q program, and provides the associated tools and templates
- Incorporates technical expertise and best practices

Mr. Rodolfo Russo

RHO, MILANO

ID number: 364201

Once the Engineering Quality Process is established, the relevant internal and external engineering organizations should be appropriately trained on the Engineering Quality Process program and associated tools (including GEP).

In addition, the Engineering Quality Process should be sufficiently flexible in its operation to obtain the benefits of standardized work practices by providing a toolbox of templates and forms without imposing unnecessary duplication where information is available in alternative formats and of an acceptable quality. The Engineering Quality Process should both facilitate and encourage the use of knowledge generated in a previous stage/step which is relevant to the C&Q activity currently being undertaken. This referencing of knowledge imposes a necessary discipline in the assessment, management, and maintenance of the knowledge, particularly on the change management practices used.

For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
---
# ISPE Baseline® Guide: Commissioning and Qualification

# 11.3 Deliverables

The main deliverable from a quality system is to ensure that systems are operating as intended as captured in documentation. A document hierarchy within the Engineering Quality Process helps to identify where the requirements/expectations are derived.

Figure 11.1 illustrates a typical document hierarchy for a quality system.

PoliciesDirectivesStandardsProceduresExecutable Documents

On the top of the hierarchy are policies/directives, which are the high-level documents that capture the expectations of the organization. The expectations for C&Q may be that the systems used to manufacture products have been qualified to be suitable for the intended purpose. These expectations may be part of an overall validation policy or detailed in an independent policy.

The next level is standards, which identify the minimum deliverables that are required for implementation of the program. The intent is to avoid different parts of the organization interpreting the intent of the policies/directives differently. For C&Q, the deliverable may be a Qualification Summary Report. Standards should:

- Reference any specific relevant regulatory authority guidance
- Be used to implement a harmonized approach throughout the organization – this should be an organization-wide model with a process for sites to learn from each other and practice a wider continual improvement path
- Limit special conditions – the standard should not make allowances for individual site preferences or commitments, but should include a process to grant exceptions after proper review and approval

The next level is procedures, which may establish steps to be followed for the implementation of standards. Procedures may be corporate or site-specific documents. Procedures may be used for multiple sites but should avoid the term global since not all sites in the organization may follow them. Examples of expected procedures are for:

- URS
- System Risk Assessment (identification of CAs/CDEs)
- DR/DQ
- Protocols/Plans
- Engineering Change Management

For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
---
# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

# Reports

The bottom level of the hierarchy is executable documents, which are the plans, test scripts, reports, etc. that document compliance to the established requirements.

In summary, the Engineering Quality Process should:

- Define how oversight by the Quality Unit on the program or project will be accomplished – this is especially important on large projects that may last for an extended period of time and may include personnel changes
- Define approvers of documentation – the Quality Unit does not need to approve every document; the documents that require Quality Unit approval are described in various chapters of this Guide (see also Section 6.4 for an example approval matrix)
- Establish the linkage to the QRM process for C&Q (System Risk Assessment as described in Chapter 4)
- Address integrated C&Q efforts – e.g., integrate the C&Q process so that one set of testing and documentation can satisfy C&Q requirements
- Address what can be done as part of start-up and how much shakedown is done before documentation starts
- Identify the level of testing and expectations for documentation – this should be commensurate with the risk to product quality; the objective is to provide confidence that overall delivery of the program delivers a working system and a quality product
- Define the vendor selection process – it should address the selection and decision-making process; then based on the information available, decide on the level of oversight and use of vendors and their documentation
- Provide a means for measuring progress and maintenance in the C&Q program – it should establish a metric control plan to measure progress
- Ensure continual improvement – senior management should be responsible for the quality system governance through management review to ensure continuing suitability and effectiveness of Engineering Quality Process

# Roles and Responsibilities

The roles and responsibilities for the Engineering Quality Process should be defined. The functions that contribute to the Engineering Quality Process are:

- Engineering: Align engineering systems to support the Engineering Quality Process
- Manufacturing:
- Mr. Rodolfo Russo (representative(s) of the group that will use the system to manufacture products) Ensure the Engineering Quality Process will be able to deliver a system suitable for the intended purpose
- Technical Support/Research: Provide technical guidance for product and process requirements and critical parameter ranges based on Basis of Design studies during product development
- Quality Unit: Ensure the Engineering Quality Process fits within the QMS and agree with the provisions of the Engineering Quality Process
- HSE: Ensure the Engineering Quality Process addresses personnel safety and impact to the environment and includes provisions to meet local, state, and federal requirements

# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

# 12 Change Management

# 12.1 Introduction

Change management processes should be established and applied throughout the lifecycle of manufacturing systems. The risk-based application of SME-led change management is one of the biggest challenges in the implementation of QRM-based C&Q as it applies the principles of ICH Q9 [3]. This Guide presents an approach which involves two modes of change management:

- Engineering Change Management (ECM)
- Quality Change Control (QCC)

Project change management that covers the elements of project scope, cost, and schedule is outside the scope of this Guide. A system is considered to be changed when it is modified, altered, added to, removed, or improved in a way that makes its functions, physical features, or performance different from what they were prior to the change. Replacing an existing part of a system with an identical one (like-for-like) may not be considered as a change. Organizations should define and follow a robust assessment process for classifying changes as like-for-like.

# 12.1.1 Purpose and Benefits

Change management is a documented program that:

- Defines what is subject to change
- Determines the risk for product impact caused by the change
- Establishes a process for initiation, review, and approval of the request for change
- Defines when the Quality Unit should be involved
- Establishes a process for evaluation of the actual impact of the change

Change management is a well-managed process for instituting changes and is needed for the following reasons:

- Ensures control of the impact on the current state of the system and the process it belongs to
- Provides a basis for peer (SME) review and approval
- Is a regulatory requirement (e.g., RHO, MILANO 21 CFR 211.100 [30] and EU GMP Volume 4 (§5.23) [31])
- Is a good business practice

Change management processes should not be (or perceived to be) an obstruction to fostering innovation and continual improvement. All stakeholders in the change management system should understand the purposes and benefits of effective change management. Efficient use of change management adds value by delivering technically sound solutions in an efficient manner and makes innovation and continual improvement less cost or time prohibitive.

For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
---
# ISPE Baseline® Guide: Commissioning and Qualification

At the same time, management of change is crucial to maintaining control of risks to product quality and patient safety. Demonstrating suitability for the intended purpose initially is not sufficient; maintaining its suitability and actually improving it over time are key concepts in the lifecycle approach.

# 12.1.2 Principles/Concepts

Figure 12.1 shows the change management application during the C&Q project execution lifecycle and illustrates the differences between the two basic types of change management processes presented in this chapter, ECM and QCC.

# Figure 12.1: Change Management Application during C&Q Project Execution Lifecycle

|Inputs|Requirements|Specify and Design|Design Qualification|Acceptance and Release|Operation and Continued Improvement|
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
|Change Management|Change Management|Change Management|Change Management|Change Management|Change Management|
|ECM during Project C&Q (Verification) complete|ECM during Project C&Q (Verification) complete|ECM during Project C&Q (Verification) complete|ECM during Project C&Q (Verification) complete|ECM during Project C&Q (Verification) complete|ECM during Project C&Q (Verification) complete|
|Subsequent changes evaluated for Quality Impact|Decommissioned|Decommissioned|Decommissioned|Decommissioned|Decommissioned|

Table 12.1 outlines the differences between ECM and QCC in more detail.

# Table 12.1: Comparison of Change Management Processes

|Engineering Change Management (ECM)|Quality Change Control (QCC)|
|---|---|
|• Managed by Engineering or Operations unit|• Managed by Quality Assurance (QA)|
|• Requires changes made to CAs/CDEs and CPPs to be approved by the Quality Unit|• Requires changes to be pre-approved by the Quality Unit prior to initiation|
|• System changes and approvals performed by appropriate SMEs and system owners|• Impact of changes on system’s qualified/validated status needs to be evaluated and documented|
|• Applicable during projects prior to acceptance and release|• Requalification needs to be approved by the Quality Unit, along with the post-change verification documentation of completion|
|• May also be applied at the site level during subsequent lifecycle management of changes to non-critical aspects, components, or functions (as determined and documented by System Risk Assessments)|• Applicable to C&Q projects following Quality Unit acceptance and release and during subsequent lifecycle management, except as specifically defined for non-critical aspects, components or functions through a System Risk Assessment|

# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

# 12.2 Engineering Change Management

ECM is a key component of the Engineering Quality Process (as described in Chapter 11). It provides the primary control mechanism within the overall Engineering Quality Process and is fundamental in supporting the execution of regulatory/quality change control for CQAs. Appropriate SMEs, process owners, and the Quality Unit (where CAs/CDEs are impacted) should be involved in the evaluation, review, and approval of the potential impact, the intended purpose of the change, and the work required to implement the change.

There are three primary areas of application for ECM:

- Provide lifecycle management for systems supporting both GMP and non-GMP regulated processes during operation
- Provide the primary change management mechanism during a project phase including work intended to support C&Q (e.g., during DR/DQ, fabrication, FAT/SAT, and verification)
- Manage and document technical changes for non-critical aspects of qualified systems

The review and approval method for the ECM system should enable effective and rapid change management that is sufficiently flexible to adapt to:

- The stage of the project and related regulatory impact
- The impact on manufacturing, maintenance, and system lifecycle

The level of documentation of the change should be commensurate with the level of risk related to the change. It is important to understand the following:

- When and where ECM may be applied within the organization
- The point in the project lifecycle at which QA-managed QCC applies

# 12.2.1 Scope and Sequence of ECM

International GMPs require that changes that have the potential to impact product quality and patient safety be handled within the PQS (refer to Chapter 15 (Appendix 1) for the regulatory basis). This drives the need to establish a defined endpoint for application of ECM to GMP and non-GMP changes within the project lifecycle; from this point forward, full application of QCC to CAs/CDEs within systems commences.

Figure 12.1 (in Section 12.1) highlights the potential benefits to be gained when applying an SME-led science and risk-based approach, by not commencing QA-managed QCC until the acceptance and release phase at the earliest. From a regulatory perspective, it is only after the acceptance and release of a system (for execution of PQ/Process Validation, if applicable, or for manufacturing for commercial use) that changes to systems with CAs/CDEs should go through QA-managed QCC.

During the operational phase of a system, ECM should continue to be applied to non-critical aspects of the system. Each change to the system should therefore be evaluated on whether CAs/CDEs are affected by the change.

# 12.2.2 Application of ECM

Table 12.2 provides examples of types of changes and the suggested change management mechanism for each type of change. These examples are provided for illustrative purposes; actual application of the change management mechanisms should be based on organization procedures.

For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.

# ISPE Baseline® Guide: Commissioning and Qualification

# Table 12.2: Example Changes and Suggested Change Management Application

|Change Description|ECM without Quality Unit Involvement|ECM with Quality Unit Approval|QCC|
|---|---|---|---|
|New system or modification to existing system impacting only non-GMP regulations (such as safety, environmental, etc.)| | | |
|Changes at any point in its lifecycle to a system supporting a GMP process but that itself does not contain CAs/CDEs (i.e., not direct impact systems)| | | |
|Changes during C&Q that do not impact CAs/CDEs or their acceptance criteria| | | |
|Changes during C&Q that modify or impact CAs/CDEs or their acceptance criteria| | | |
|Changes to any document, specification, or requirement previously approved by the Quality Unit| | | |
|Acceptance and release for use of systems containing CAs/CDEs| | | |
|Changes after acceptance and release to a system that contains CAs/CDEs (qualified system) subject to local Quality Unit procedures| | | |
|Changes after acceptance and release to non-critical aspects of qualified systems, if justified by risk assessment and subject to local Quality Unit procedures and approvals| | | |

Note:

- The differences between QCC and ECM with Quality Unit Approval are:
- QCC requires Quality Unit pre-approval of a change
- ECM with Quality Unit approval only requires Quality Unit approval for ECM-managed changes prior to acceptance and release or after approval of the project-related activity in question

# 12.3 Change Management during C&Q Projects

Before new systems are used for commercial or clinical operations, there is no risk to the patient associated with changes to design or function during the C&Q or project lifecycle. There is therefore no regulatory requirement for Quality Unit approval prior to implementation of a change.

There is, however, potential risk to the successful delivery of product and process user requirements and the risk control strategies determined and approved as part of the approved System Risk Assessment (as described in Chapter 4). The Quality Unit should approve, prior to verification testing, any changes to the design or intended function or acceptance criteria associated with CAs/CDEs (including any new CAs/CDEs driven by changes in design).

In addition to maintaining the intended design after DR/DQ, ECM also plays a role in ensuring the integrity of data and evidence generated during C&Q. Fixes and repairs during C&Q have the potential to affect and invalidate testing and other evidence previously generated and compiled against the original configuration.

# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

Test results from commissioning that do not meet acceptance criteria require:

- Documented description of the incident
- Description of corrective action(s) taken
- Retest or re-verification of results following the correction
- Assessment of the impact of the corrective action on any test results or other evidence already collected, and determination of any associated retesting requirements

A note should be made in the C&Q record and action taken to correct the item to meet the acceptance criteria. If the acceptance criteria cannot be met after corrective actions have been made, an SME should evaluate the issue and determine if it can be accepted as-is. A note should be made in the commissioning record to indicate why the item is acceptable as-is.

For more information on handling of discrepancies, refer to Section 7.4.

# 12.4 Change Management during Operation

After acceptance and release and after the system is placed into production use, any changes to the qualified system has the potential to affect product quality and patient safety. GMP regulations usually require Quality Unit pre-approval (approval prior to non-emergency implementation) of changes with the potential to affect product quality (refer to Chapter 15 (Appendix 1) for the regulatory basis). When using a risk-based approach, QA-managed QCC should be applied when the change impacts CAs/CDEs.

After acceptance and release, there are scenarios that allow for application of ECM without Quality Unit involvement. Organizations should establish procedures that state the specific requirements for use of ECM within the organization or facility. Routine operations that may allow for application of ECM without Quality Unit involvement include, for example:

- Changes to non-GMP aspects of systems
- Changes to non-critical aspects of systems (as established by the System Risk Assessment)
- Certain categories of changes, such as like-for-like, as documented in the completed verification and approved acceptance and release documentation

When QCC is used to manage changes, a log or tracking system should be used to maintain change histories for systems with CAs/CDEs. IT tools providing this functionality may be commercially available. A complete record of all changes to qualified systems should be able to be retrieved on demand.

# 12.5 Engineering Change Management Process

ECM is defined as a process by which qualified representative(s) reviews proposed changes for its impact, approves or denies the requests, and manages and tracks their implementation. ECM is a good engineering practice to effectively manage project execution and the associated costs and schedule.

An ECM process may contain the following attributes:

- Recorded name of the originator and date

# ISPE Baseline® Guide: Commissioning and Qualification

- Description of the change, including the affected system or area and intended purpose
- Assessment of the potential impact of the change on the following:
- Quality requirements
- Project schedule
- Project budget (relative to the latest approved budget)
- System scope, design, or performance requirements (including safety, operability, reliability, construction, commissioning, operations, and maintenance)
- Other systems
- Engineering documentation
- Qualification documents (including system classification as described in Chapter 3)
- Notification of the proposed change to stakeholders
- Record of the approval or denial of the proposed change
- Tracking through completion

A key consideration for successful implementation of a change management system is to apply a flexible approach. No one change management system will fit all situations. The change management system should be able to adapt to fit all stages of the lifecycle. Elements of the change management system to be applied in the project lifecycle are as follows:

# Project Conceptual Phase

There needs to be a clear definition of which documents are controlled by QA and where QA is only involved on request. Key product and process knowledge documents (such as the URS, pilot scale process reports, front end studies/design, System Risk Assessment and Validation Master Plan (VMP)) provide the basis for basic design and the requirements for CQAs and CPPs. Changes to these key documents should be addressed using ECM with Quality Unit approval. Change management needs to ensure these changes are assessed, implemented, and approved as to their potential impact on product quality and patient safety.

# Design Phase

During this stage, change management should be applied in alignment with the principles of GEP, with approval by SMEs. The mechanics of proposing, approving, implementing, and tracking changes should be simple since the design phase is very fluid and changes may be frequent. Lead disciplines, including the system owner, should be notified of approved changes. It is important to recognize the difference between design development and change.

The final design (including the implemented changes) will be assessed by DR/DQ to verify that the documented user requirements and associated design intent are met. At the completion of the design phase, DR/DQ is completed. At this point, ECM is applied to GEP changes, and ECM with Quality Unit approval is applied to changes in CAs/CDEs (with Quality Unit approval).

# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

• Construction, C&Q Phase: During this phase, for large projects, the site and project are primarily under the control of a contract organization. In this scenario, changes should be implemented using the contract organization’s change management policy with input by the owner.

During construction, many changes are driven by the reality of the installed environment versus the design intent. All changes to the design should be recorded as part of GEP. Those changes that impact quality require further review and approval.

Typical changes during this phase relate to:

- Field inspections and testing performance
- Controlled by ECM
- May require retesting post-change
- New forms/results appended to document
- Retest results are reviewed for conformance
- Departures from specification, which typically fall into three categories:
- Readily corrected (e.g., pipe labels): Correct, reinspect, and document
- Corrected later (or impacting other inspections/tests): Use discrepancy/punch list or change management
- Cannot correct: Needs to be evaluated and acted on by the technical reviewer
- Deviations – only occur during performance testing when a product and process user requirement is not met or a quality risk control (CAs/CDEs) is not met through its feature, function, or component failure. The following steps need to be taken:
- Documentation and evaluation by the Quality and Manufacturing Units
- Determination of an assignable cause
- Corrective action undertaken including the elements of definition, approval, implementation, test, confirmation, and documentation

Interim release may be considered if there is no quality impact.

Correction of deviations should be addressed in the following preferred order:

1. Design change
2. Installation change
3. Operational change

Non-correctable deviations may require an SOP or other procedural change (i.e., procedural control) to compensate for the shortcoming.

# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

# 13 Good Documentation Practice for C&Q

# 13.1 Introduction

The goal of Good Documentation Practice is to create clear, concise documents that a knowledgeable peer may review without questions. Good Documentation Practice supports data integrity. Documents should be:

- Attributable
- Legible
- Contemporaneous
- Original
- Accurate

# 13.1.1 Scope and Purpose

This chapter applies to all C&Q documentation, including test documents and reports. In this chapter, GEP documents are considered to be the engineering records used to support commissioning and GMP records are considered to be the documentation supporting qualification. Table 13.1 provides more details on the differences between testing and the documentation standards for GEP versus GMP applications.

|Table 13.1: Differences Between Commissioning versus Qualification Testing and Documentation|Table 13.1: Differences Between Commissioning versus Qualification Testing and Documentation|
|---|
|Commissioning (GEP)|Qualification (GMP)|
|Iterative testing and adjustment may be performed with a permanent record of the final results (e.g., HVAC system balancing).|Protocols are followed exactly, and all events are recorded as they occur. All deviations/exceptions and each retest are documented.|
|Engineering standard testing practices (e.g., ISO [18], NEBB [28]), including test plans, can be followed. Acceptance criteria may be in the approved specifications.|Custom protocols with pre-approved acceptance criteria are used for execution of test scripts.|
|Engineering judgment of acceptability is used to verify specification and design for non-critical aspects (e.g., loop pressure in a WFI distribution system).|Quality Unit reviews and approves acceptability to verify the system is suitable for the intended purpose, which includes confirmation of CAs/CDEs.|
|Generally accepted Good Documentation Practice, as described in Section 13.3.2, can be used.|Organization/site specific practices are required for GMP records such as qualification documents.|
|Level of rigor is aligned to control of risk (equipment functionality).|Level of rigor is aligned to control of risk (GMP compliance). Note: No product is at risk during C&Q of systems.|

The level of approval also differs for the two documentation categories:

- Qualification testing strategy and acceptance are approved by the Quality Unit. Qualification testing and documents may or may not be approved by the Quality Unit depending on the approved testing strategy.
- Commissioning documents are typically not approved by the Quality Unit. The testing strategy and acceptance criteria are generally approved by Engineering.

For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.

# ISPE Baseline® Guide: Commissioning and Qualification

The purpose of this chapter is to provide specific guidance on Good Documentation Practice so that documentation generated during commissioning (GEP) testing can be used to meet the requirements for qualification (GMP).

# 13.1.2 Requirements for Vendor Documentation to Support Qualification

When the decision is made to use vendor documentation to support system qualification of CAs/CDEs, the justification should be documented and approved by SMEs and the Quality Unit. Considerations should be made for the following aspects:

- Vendor assessment (as described in Chapter 10)
- Acceptable vendor quality system
- Vendor technical capability
- Vendor application of GEP such that information obtained from the vendor will be accurate and suitable to meet the purpose of qualification

Refer to Chapter 7 for details on testing and verification.

# 13.2 C&Q Document Development Basics

Testing and verification documentation are usually written in the local language. Summary documents are generally in English to facilitate regulatory review.

The use of forms/templates is encouraged to promote consistency. Forms/templates can be approved once when created and then utilized multiple times without pre-approval if the relevant content is not changed. Where this approach is used, there should be a procedure to ensure that the form/template used for data entry is current at the time of use.

When forms/templates are used, the SME should review the form to ensure that it includes all of the required testing for the system being verified; if additional tests are required, the form/template should be supplemented with additional testing.

Test scripts may be approved individually and assembled into a test plan/report. If test data sheets are used to supplement test scripts, they should be approved in accordance with the requirements defined in the test plan.

The test procedures should be clear and unambiguous such that the results are repeatable and reproducible, as explained below:

- Repeatable: The same result would be achieved if the same procedure is repeated by the same trained and qualified personnel.
- Reproducible: The test procedures should be performed the same way each time and the same result would be achieved by different trained and qualified personnel.

The test document should indicate the order that the cases are to be executed. Acceptance criteria needs to be established and it should be clear that the tests demonstrate that the acceptance criteria have been met.

# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

# 13.3 Document Execution

# 13.3.1 Test Execution Basics

The basic flow of GEP testing to verify that acceptance criteria have been established and have been met is shown in Figure 13.1.

# Figure 13.1: Basic Flow Chart for GEP Testing

|Approved Test Methodology and Conditions|Approved Acceptance Criteria|
|---|---|
|Execute Test|Repeat Test|
|Results documented and acceptance criteria met?| |
|No|Accept Test Result|
|Yes| |

# 13.3.1.1 Executor Qualification and Signature Log

Individuals who enter information in a test document (i.e., executors) must possess the education, training, experience, or any combination thereof to perform their work [32] and record their printed name, signature, and initials in a signature log. An executor cannot review or approve his/her own work.

# 13.3.1.2 Contemporaneous Records

Documents should be completed as work is performed. Results should be recorded at the time of activity. Backdating is not permitted. The steps within a test case should be executed in order unless specified otherwise. Provide an explanation for any step or task performed out of sequence.

If a test script takes more than one calendar day to execute, indicate where testing stopped and initial and date this entry at the appropriate test step.

# 13.3.1.3 Transcribed Data

It is permissible to transcribe data from one form to another (e.g., printed tabular summary of manual entries or manually transcribing single entries from computer generated printouts). Transcribed data should be signed and dated. The date of the actual data should be included. Transcribed data should be checked by a second person. If data is transcribed, the raw data should be included – labeled, initialed and dated.

For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
---
# ISPE Baseline® Guide: Commissioning and Qualification

# 13.3.1.4 Printouts

Test steps should indicate when printouts are to be used. Printouts should be attached to the test plan/report appendix or contained in the appropriate reference file. See also Section 13.4.

Where the output is from a thermal printer (e.g., for a particle counter), a photocopy should be used, signed and dated as a true copy by the individual responsible for the test. A footnote should be annotated in the test document to reference the location of the original.

# 13.3.1.5 Supporting Documentation

Attach or reference supporting documentation as necessary. Original data sheets and checklists should be available for review until the transcribed document has been accepted and approved. Refer to organization requirements or relevant regional regulations regarding any specific documentation requirements prior to destroying supporting documentation for GMP records/documents.

# 13.3.1.6 Proper Handling of Documents

It is understood and accepted that testing documents will experience some wear and tear typical of field executed documentation. However, care should be taken to protect documents from spills, excessive folding, or other physical damage.

# 13.3.1.7 Document Changes

Changes to approved documents require approval by representatives of the original approving groups.

# 13.3.2 Good Test Execution Documentation

The typical requirements for recording a GEP document should include:

- Use permanent, non-fading, non-smearing ink.
- - The use of blue or black ink is preferred. Traditionally, blue/black ink was required due to limitations in copying technology; current technology commonly allows for copying of all colors. The use of alternative colors is also permitted.
- If colors are used for making up redline drawings, the definition of the meaning of the colors needs to be clear.

In some cases, GEP field documentation (e.g., balance reports, isometrics) is recorded in pencil. A photocopy of these documents should be used, signed, and dated as a true copy by the individual responsible for the test.
- Ditto marks (") or arrows (↓) down on a column to indicate duplicate entries in subsequent steps are not permitted.
- Writing over an existing entry is not permitted (see Section 13.5 for corrections).
- There should be no scribbling on the record. When adding notes, use unique alpha/numeric identifiers to trace each entry to the corresponding footnote.
- If a signature log is maintained (with the signature, initials, and printed name of each individual that recorded in the document), initials are acceptable in lieu of signatures everywhere except for document approvals.

For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
---
# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

- Initial and date any handwritten changes to changes or comments. Where the reason for the change is not clear, there should be an explanatory note (see Section 7.4 for discrepancy management).
- Initial and date each page with an entry at least once.
- Date format is country/organization specific but needs to be unambiguous. DD-MMM-YYYY is preferred, for example 30-MAY-2019.
- Time format is organization/site specific but needs to be unambiguous. Record time by using the 24-hour clock (e.g., 1425) or am/pm (e.g., 2:25 pm), as applicable.
- For significant figures:
- Use a zero before a decimal point if there is no other digit.
- Record all figures as shown on the measuring instrument or readout.
- Ensure calculations indicate the correct unit of measure and use the correct number of significant digits.
- For final data that is reported, round to the number of places that agrees with the stated specification or limit.
- For quantitative results, record the number. Examples of unacceptable entries include:
- “Pass”
- “Fail”
- “As Expected”
- “Same”
- “OK”
- “Yes”
- Checkmark “ ”
- Record the observed system response from the test step execution in the results column. Use unambiguous terminology; it is not necessary to record verbatim the content of the expected results in the results column.
- Line out, initial, and date all blank spaces. Be careful to distinguish the use of “N/A” (Not Applicable) and “N/AV” (Not Available). “N/A” should only be used prior to approval to execute. “N/A” after document approval generally requires an explanation, initials and date. All unused fields on pages with original signatures or data should be marked with “N/A”, “None”, or equivalent, and initialed and dated.
- Where entire sections, columns, rows, or whole pages are not utilized, it is acceptable to cross out the unused area with a single diagonal line. Initial and date the cross-out.
- Data superseded by a subsequent test or inspection (e.g., after a repair or correction) may be deleted. The final record should be annotated to indicate if a repair, engineering change or correction has been made.
- Initials/signatures are not required for each test step; where a test cannot be completed in one calendar day or when there is a different person responsible for testing, the final step should be initialed/signed and dated so that the executor responsible for each test step is clearly identifiable.

# ISPE Baseline® Guide: Commissioning and Qualification

• Test reports should include a summary of the results including a conclusion that the system is or is not suitable for the intended purpose.

• The completed record should be able to be copied/scanned legibly.

# 13.4 Attachments and Supporting Documentation

The integrity of all original records should be maintained. Printouts should indicate data dates. Data dates should coincide with test step execution. It is acceptable for printout dates to be later.

All screen printouts and printed reports should include the following labeling information:

- Protocol number
- Unique attachment number
- Test script ID
- Step number
- Tester’s initials
- Date
- Pagination: Page X of Y, where X represents the page number and Y represents the number of pages in the attachment

Every page should include Page X of Y pagination. If records are paginated as described and have a unique identifier, it is acceptable to initial and date only the first and last pages. Multiple attachments should have a summary cover sheet.

Use of sticky notes or other unofficial media for recording original data or information should not be permitted.

# 13.5 Corrections

Mistakes and errors happen. Do not erase, overwrite, or corrupt original recorded data. Correction fluid, correction tape, pencil, and erasable ink should be avoided. Data should not be altered, obliterated, erased, or overwritten.

Corrections are acceptable when:

- Correcting test procedures or instructions that do not change the intent of the step
- Correcting typographical errors
- Correcting test/sampling instructions
- Correcting test data with no impact on interpretation

# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

Corrections can be handled in the following manner:

- Mark a single line through the error
- The cross-out should permit the original information to be read
- Always cross out the full number/word rather than an individual digit/letter when making a correction
- Record the correct information near the error and initial and date when the correction was made
- If the reason for the change is not obvious, add a unique alpha/numeric identifier to reference the change, and record the justiﬁcation on the same page using the reference alpha/numeric identifier.

Situations may arise where the person needed for making a correction is no longer available. Any corrections made by a different person than the one making the initial entry needs to be compliant with the document/protocol and/or site procedures.

Refer to Section 7.4 for more information regarding discrepancy resolution.

# 13.6 Document Review and Approval

Approvers should have the education, training, experience, or any combination thereof to review and/or approve the record [32]. Quality review of commissioning documents can be limited to the product impact relevant tests by executing pre-approved test scripts or by assembling specific test scripts into a separate test plan.

Reviewers and approvers should ensure that all appendices and registers have been filled out in accordance with the applicable procedures and that the document is complete, legible, and unambiguous. All test results should conform to established acceptance criteria; all discrepancies should be closed and approvals for discrepancy closure should have been obtained.

# 13.7 Storage, Retrieval, and Retention of Documents

Methods of document storage should be established, as dictated by organization/site policies and procedures and in a manner that allows documents to be located and retrieved within a reasonable time period, as required. The methods should include access control and tracking. The storage area should be secure.

Copies and original documents should be labeled as such. Executed documents can be scanned after final approval and archived as electronic records in a validated document management system. Once archiving is completed, the hard copy can be destroyed. Retention of records should follow organization/site specific records management policy.

Check the specific requirements of Mr. Rodolfo Russo authorities for the regions that the site will supply before destroying GMP records/documents. RHO, MILANO

# 13.8 Disaster Recovery

There should be a system in place to handle the reconstruction of records. All measures should be taken to recover the original document. For a record that is lost or destroyed and not recoverable, a copy of the original record is acceptable as the official copy, which should be approved by Management.

Note: Storage and management of documents during the execution of a project should be defined to minimize risk of loss and/or damage.

# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

# 14 Strategies for Implementation of Science and Risk-Based C&Q Process

# 14.1 Introduction

This chapter describes strategies how organizations can effectively transition to the science and risk-based C&Q
process described in this Guide. Implementing change successfully often requires high-level sponsorship due to the
numerous stakeholders impacted.

The key to a successful transition is to recognize that there are multiple ways that the regulatory expectations can
be met; adopting the science and risk-based approach does not invalidate current work or existing documentation. It
should be noted that any process used for C&Q is expected to be science and risk-based.

Qualification documents represent a picture of the system at a point in time; this information changes with time
and there are quality systems in place to manage this change (including associated revision of the qualification
and engineering record documents), such as change management, maintenance, calibration, and periodic review.
Therefore, existing documentation can remain acceptable to provide the documented evidence that the system is
suitable for the intended purpose.

Figure 14.1 provides a flow chart of the suggested steps for implementation of the science and risk-based C&Q
approach described in this Guide.

For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.

# ISPE Baseline® Guide: Commissioning and Qualification

# Figure 14.1: Implementation Flow Chart for Science and Risk-Based C&Q Approach

Do you want to implement the science and risk-based C&Q approach described in this Guide?

Yes

Use organization/site established science and risk-based C&Q approachDefine the new approachImplement engineering and quality processes requiredConduct training
# Major Change

(major change to an existing qualified system or existing system with potential compliance gaps)

Confirm qualification is still required
# Minor Change

(minor change to existing system with good qualification package)

Complete risk assessment only for aspects impacted by the change, not the whole system
# New system

Follow the new process

# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

# 14.2 Preparing for Implementation

The suggested steps for implementation preparation are described in more detail as follows:

1. Define the organization approach:
This Guide describes a suggested approach for organizations to adopt, but it may need to be tailored to fit the specific structure of the organization.
2. Evaluate the organizational maturity:
It is useful to perform a documented assessment of organizational maturity, considering the quality systems and GEP, to determine the degree and pace of organizational transition to a science and risk-based C&Q approach. The assessment scope is based on the process that the organization intends to implement and should consider all relevant aspects (e.g., experience, personnel resources, organizational culture). Examples of criteria for the relevant departments include:

- Research and Development/Process Development (i.e., the department that provides the science and process knowledge for which the C&Q program is based): Does this department have robust documentation and clear communication channels to provide the data to enable the process?
- Quality: Is the Quality Unit open to an alternative approach to C&Q and to the different documentation deliverables?
- Engineering: Is the engineering department ready to deliver documents per Good Documentation Practice? Do they have adequate change management systems in place? Is there adequate document storage?
- System Owners: Are the system owners adequately trained to perform the necessary reviews?
3. Develop an implementation plan:
A critical requirement for successful implementation is to have a well-defined implementation strategy with support from senior management. The use of initial small-scale proof of concept projects can help to identify any program aspects that require augmentation for a specific organization.
4. Develop supporting quality systems and associated procedures:
The procedures defining the engineering, quality, and documentation standards, as covered in this Guide, should be developed.
5. Train staff on the approach:
An important aspect for implementation is to provide overview training and ensure there is a good understanding of the key concepts. Considerations should also be made for how to present the approach to regulatory inspectors.
6. Train staff on the procedures:
The relevant staff should be sufficiently trained on the new procedures and have a good understanding of the overall program and its goals.

For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
---
# 14.3 Approaches for Implementation

For new projects or facilities, the use of a champion for the new approach is recommended. This individual helps with the transition and implementation of the new approach and should have a good understanding of the process concepts/goals.

While implementation is more straightforward for new systems (for which there are no existing procedures/documentation), organizations also need to determine how the approach can be applied to a change on an existing qualified system. The most cost-effective way for implementation of the risk-based approach is to supplement the legacy approach as gaps are identified or changes to the system are made, thus maximizing the use of existing documentation.

A best practice approach would be to first assess if the system requires qualification – does the system have the potential to impact product quality? (see Chapter 3 for system classification criteria)

- If it does not have the potential to impact product quality, then recategorize the system and commission the changes.
- If it has the potential to impact product quality, then it requires qualification:
- Review the existing documentation to determine if it meets current expectations
- If it does not, then qualify the system using the new approach, and remediate any gaps identified.

Table 14.1 shows the typical situations that may arise when implementing the science and risk-based C&Q approach within organizations with legacy systems, and the suggested approach.

# Commissioning and Qualification

|Step|Major Change (major change to an existing qualified system or existing system with potential compliance gaps)|Minor Change (minor change to existing system with good qualification package)|New System|
|---|---|---|---|
|URS|• Review process requirements and gaps • Identify CAs (based on risk) and proposed technical (CDEs) and procedural controls|Requirements definition of the change control is required to describe the change|Required|
|System Risk Assessment|Risk assessment is required to determine the impact of the change|Required|Required|
|DR/DQ| |Review the design to ensure change requirements are met|Required|
|C&Q Plan|• Develop the plan to describe the approach|Content is typically included in the change control|Required|
|Test Plan| |Content is typically contained in change control (depending on complexity of testing required)|Required|
|Testing|• Review existing qualification documentation to confirm technical controls (CDEs) that were identified based on risk have been tested • Supplement with additional testing if required • Review procedures to confirm procedural controls are in place • Update if required|Required to address the change|Required|
|Procedures (operations and maintenance)|Revise to address the change as required|Revise to address the change as required|Required|
|Acceptance and Release (Qualification Summary Report)|Required|Recorded and approved through closeout of the change control|Required|

Note: Any of these aspects may be combined depending on the magnitude and complexity of the project.

Refer to Chapter 28 (Appendix 14) for an organizational maturity model and Chapter 29 (Appendix 15) for an approach to qualifying legacy systems or systems with inadequate qualification.

# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

# Appendix 1

# 15  Appendix 1 – Regulatory Basis

This Guide is intended to assist organizations in achieving compliance with regulatory expectations regarding C&Q. This appendix references key regulatory and guidance documents, highlighting text associated with the topics covered in each chapter of this Guide. These lists are not intended to be all inclusive. It is recognized that there are ongoing developments with regulatory guidelines and this Guide reflects an understanding of them as of the publication date.

The regulatory expectations for C&Q are primarily described in the following documents:

- EU GMP Annex 15 Qualification and Validation (October 2015) [1]
- Pharmaceutical Inspection Co-operation Scheme (PIC/S) Annex 15 Qualification and validation (July 2018) [33]⁵
- US FDA Guidance for Industry: Process Validation: General Principles and Practices (January 2011) [2]
- ICH Q8 Pharmaceutical Development (August 2009) [4]
- ICH Q9 Quality Risk Management (November 2005) [3]
- ICH Q10 Pharmaceutical Quality System (June 2008) [9]
- EU GMP Annex 3 Manufacture of Radiopharmaceuticals (September 2008) [34]
- EU GMP Annex 11 Computerised Systems (June 2011) [21]

Note: Computer system validation is covered in ISPE GAMP® 5: A Risk-Based Approach to Compliant GxP Computerized Systems [6]; detailed regulatory expectations for computerized systems are not listed here. In addition, the FDA 2011 Process Validation Guidance [2] notes that ASTM E2500-13 [5] may be a useful reference. The principles described in ASTM E2500-13 [5] are consistent with the regulatory references and provide additional clarity on the topics of this Guide.

# 15.1     Chapter 1: Introduction

This Document is licensed to Mr. Rodolfo Russo RHO, MILANO ID number: 364201

The following texts detail the regulatory basis for employing a science and risk-based approach to qualification and validation over the lifecycle of a drug:

- “The CGMP regulations require that manufacturing processes be designed and controlled to assure that in-process materials and the finished product meet predetermined quality requirements and do so consistently and reliably.” [2]
- “It is a GMP requirement that manufacturers control the critical aspects of their particular operations through qualification and validation over the life cycle of the product and process.” [1, 33]
- “A quality risk management approach should be applied throughout the lifecycle of a medicinal product. As part of a quality risk management system, decisions on the scope and extent of qualification and validation should be based on a justified and documented risk assessment of the facilities, equipment, utilities and processes.” [1, 33]

5 PIC/S adopted the EU GMP Annex 15 [1] (PIC/S Annex 15 [33]), and from Version 12 onwards, they are identical documents.
