# ISPE Baseline® Guide: Appendix 1

# Commissioning and Qualification

- “Focusing exclusively on qualification efforts without also understanding the manufacturing process and associated variations may not lead to adequate assurance of quality.” [2]
- “Qualification activities should consider all stages from initial development of the user requirements specification through to the end of use of the equipment, facility, utility or system.” [1, 33]
- “[Facilities, systems, utilities and equipment] used for process validation should be qualified and test methods should be validated.” [1, 33]
- “Process validation should establish whether all quality attributes and process parameters which are considered important for ensuring the validated state and acceptable product quality can be consistently met by the process. The basis by which process parameters and quality attributes were identified as being critical or non-critical should be clearly documented, taking into account the results of any risk assessment activities.” [1, 33]
- “Quality by Design (QbD): A systematic approach to development that begins with predefined objectives and emphasizes product and process understanding and process control, based on sound science and quality risk management.” [4]
- Regarding radiopharmaceuticals: “The principles of qualification and validation should be applied to the manufacturing of radiopharmaceuticals and a risk management approach should be used to determine the extent of qualification/validation, focusing on a combination of Good Manufacturing Practice and Radiation Protection.” [34]
- Regarding computerized systems: “A computerised system is a set of software and hardware components which together fulfill certain functionalities. The application should be validated; IT infrastructure should be qualified.” [21]
- “[T]here should be appropriate quality oversight over the whole validation life cycle.” [1, 33]

# 15.2 Chapter 2: User Requirements Specification

It is a regulatory expectation that facilities, systems, utilities, and equipment used for product manufacturing are suitable for the intended purpose. The URS defines the intended purpose of facilities, systems, utilities, and equipment and is addressed as follows:

- “The specification for equipment, facilities, utilities or systems should be defined in a URS and/or a functional specification. The essential elements of quality need to be built in at this stage and any GMP risks mitigated to an acceptable level. The URS should be a point of reference throughout the validation life cycle.” [1, 33]
- “The term qualification refers to activities undertaken to demonstrate that utilities and equipment are suitable for their intended use and perform properly.” [2]
- “The requirements of the user requirements specification should be verified during the design qualification.” [1, 33]
- “Prior to installation, equipment should be confirmed to comply with the URS/ functional specification at the vendor site, if applicable.” [1, 33]

# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

# Appendix 1

# 15.3 Chapter 3: System Classification

The expectation for qualification and validation is based on whether equipment has a direct impact on product quality
(consistently delivers a product that meets its quality attributes). Direct impact systems have CDEs (CAs) involved
with the control of CPPs related to product CQAs. System classification is a tool for making this determination.

System classification is addressed as follows:

- “It is a GMP requirement that manufacturers control the critical aspects of their particular operations through
qualification and validation over the life cycle of the product and process.” [1, 33]
- “All attributes and parameters should be evaluated in terms of their roles in the process and impact on the
product or in-process material, and reevaluated as new information becomes available.” [2]
- “Qualification of utilities and equipment generally includes the following activities:
- Selecting utilities and equipment construction materials, operating principles, and performance
characteristics based on whether they are appropriate for their specific uses.” [2]

# 15.4 Chapter 4: System Risk Assessments

The System Risk Assessment is used in this Guide for two purposes:

- To determine the scope and extent of qualification
- To verify that the risks to product quality posed by the facilities, systems, utilities, and equipment are adequately
addressed by the design

The following regulatory requirements and guidance apply:

- “A quality risk management approach should be applied throughout the lifecycle of a medicinal product. As part of
a quality risk management system, decisions on the scope and extent of [qualification and validation] should be
based on a justified and documented risk assessment of the facilities, equipment, utilities and processes.” [1, 33]
- “A quality risk management approach should be used for qualification and validation activities. In light of
increased knowledge and understanding from any changes during the project phase or during commercial
production, the risk assessments should be repeated, as required. The way in which risk assessments are used
to support qualification and validation activities should be clearly documented.” [1, 33]
- “FDA encourages the use of modern pharmaceutical development concepts, quality risk management, and
quality systems at all stages of the manufacturing process lifecycle.” [2]
- “With a lifecycle approach to process validation that employs risk based decision making throughout that
lifecycle, the perception of criticality as a continuum rather than a binary state is more useful. All attributes and
parameters should be evaluated in terms of their roles in the process and impact on the product or in-process
material, and reevaluated as new information becomes available. The degree of control over those attributes
or parameters should be commensurate with their risk to the process and process output. In other words, a
higher degree of control is appropriate for attributes or parameters that pose a higher risk. The Agency [FDA]
recognizes that terminology usage can vary and expects that each manufacturer will communicate the meaning
and intent of its terminology and categorization to the Agency.” [2]

# ISPE Baseline® Guide: Appendix 1

# Commissioning and Qualification

- “Process controls address variability to assure quality of the product. Controls can consist of material analysis and equipment monitoring at significant processing points (§ 211.110(c)). Decisions regarding the type and extent of process controls can be aided by earlier risk assessments, then enhanced and improved as process experience is gained.” [2]
- “Qualification of utilities and equipment can be covered under individual plans or as part of an overall project plan. The plan should consider the requirements of use and can incorporate risk management to prioritize certain activities and to identify a level of effort in both the performance and documentation of qualification activities.” [2]
- “In relation to pharmaceuticals, although there are a variety of stakeholders, including patients and medical practitioners as well as government and industry, the protection of the patient by managing the risk to quality should be considered of prime importance.” [3]
- “It is neither always appropriate nor always necessary to use a formal risk management process (using recognized tools and/or internal procedures e.g., standard operating procedures). The use of informal risk management processes (using empirical tools and/or internal procedures) can also be considered acceptable.” [3]
- “The level of effort, formality and documentation of the quality risk management process should be commensurate with the level of risk.” [3]
- “Quality risk management activities are usually, but not always, undertaken by interdisciplinary teams. When teams are formed, they should include experts from the appropriate areas (e.g., quality unit, business development, engineering, regulatory affairs, production operations, sales and marketing, legal, statistics and clinical) in addition to individuals who are knowledgeable about the quality risk management process.” [3]
- “The degree of rigor and formality of quality risk management should reflect available knowledge and be commensurate with the complexity and/or criticality of the issue to be addressed.” [3]
- “FMEA [Failure Mode Effects Analysis] can be applied to equipment and facilities and might be used to analyze a manufacturing operation and its effect on product or process. It identifies elements/operations within the system that render it vulnerable. The output/results of FMEA can be used as a basis for design or further analysis or to guide resource deployment.” [3]
- “PHA [Preliminary Hazard Analysis] might be useful when analyzing existing systems or prioritizing hazards where circumstances prevent a more extensive technique from being used. It can be used for product, process and facility design as well as to evaluate the types of hazards for the general product type, then the product class, and finally the specific product.” [3]

# The following texts from regulatory references apply to DR and DQ:

- “The CGMP regulations require that manufacturing processes be designed and controlled to assure that in-process materials and the finished product meet predetermined quality requirements and do so consistently and reliably.” [2]
- “Proper design of a manufacturing facility is required under part 211, subpart C, of the CGMP regulations on Buildings and Facilities. It is essential that activities performed to assure proper facility design and commissioning precede PPQ [Process Performance Qualification].” [2]

# Appendix 1

• “The next element in the qualification of equipment, facilities, utilities, or systems is DQ where the compliance of the design with GMP should be demonstrated and documented.” [1, 33]

There is minimal regulatory guidance on how specifically to perform a GMP design review. Refer to ASTM E2500-13 [5] for additional detailed industry best practice on this topic.

# 15.6 Chapter 6: C&Q Planning

Planning is critical to the C&Q program. Planning is also a regulatory expectation. The following texts from regulatory references apply to C&Q planning:

• “Project plans, along with the full support of senior management, are essential elements for success.” [2]

• “All qualification and validation activities should be planned and take the life cycle of facilities, equipment, utilities, process and product into consideration.” [1, 33]

• “All studies should be planned and conducted according to sound scientific principles, appropriately documented, and approved in accordance with the established procedure appropriate for the stage of the lifecycle.” [2]

• “Qualification of utilities and equipment can be covered under individual plans or as part of an overall project plan. The plan should consider the requirements of use and can incorporate risk management to prioritize certain activities and to identify a level of effort in both the performance and documentation of qualification activities. The plan should identify the following items:

1. the studies or tests to use,
2. the criteria appropriate to assess outcomes,
3. the timing of qualification activities,
4. the responsibilities of relevant departments and the quality unit, and
5. the procedures for documenting and approving the qualification.

The project plan should also include the firm’s requirements for the evaluation of changes. Qualification activities should be documented and summarized in a report with conclusions that address criteria in the plan. The quality control unit must review and approve the qualification plan and report (§ 211.22).” [2]

• “The key elements of the site qualification and validation programme should be clearly defined and documented in a validation master plan (VMP) or equivalent document.

The VMP or equivalent document should define the qualification/validation system and include or reference information on at least the following:

1. Qualification and Validation policy;
2. The organisational structure including roles and responsibilities for qualification and validation activities;
3. Summary of the facilities, equipment, systems, processes on site and the qualification and validation status;
4. Change control and deviation management for qualification and validation;
5. Guidance on developing acceptance criteria;

vii. The qualification and validation strategy, including requalification, where applicable. For large and complex projects, planning takes on added importance and separate validation plans may enhance clarity.” [1, 33]

# 15.7 Chapter 7: C&Q Testing and Documentation

There is an abundance of guidance information on testing and documentation to support C&Q. The FDA Process Validation Guidance [2] states the following:

- “Throughout the product lifecycle, various studies can be initiated to discover, observe, correlate, or confirm information about the product and process. All studies should be planned and conducted according to sound scientific principles, appropriately documented, and approved in accordance with the established procedure appropriate for the stage of the lifecycle.”
- “During the process qualification (PQ) stage of process validation, the process design is evaluated to determine if it is capable of reproducible commercial manufacture. This stage has two elements: (1) design of the facility and qualification of the equipment and utilities and (2) process performance qualification (PPQ). During Stage 2, CGMP-compliant procedures must be followed.”
- “It is essential that activities performed to assure proper facility design and commissioning precede PPQ.”
- “Qualification of utilities and equipment generally includes the following activities:
- Selecting utilities and equipment construction materials, operating principles, and performance characteristics based on whether they are appropriate for their specific uses.
- Verifying that utility systems and equipment are built and installed in compliance with the design specifications (e.g., built as designed with proper materials, capacity, and functions, and properly connected and calibrated).
- Verifying that utility systems and equipment operate in accordance with the process requirements in all anticipated operating ranges. This should include challenging the equipment or system functions while under load comparable to that expected during routine production. It should also include the performance of interventions, stoppage, and start-up as is expected during routine production. Operating ranges should be shown capable of being held as long as would be necessary during routine production.”
- “The process performance qualification (PPQ) is the second element of Stage 2, process qualification. The PPQ combines the actual facility, utilities, equipment (each now qualified), and the trained personnel with the commercial manufacturing process, control procedures, and components to produce commercial batches. A successful PPQ will confirm the process design and demonstrate that the commercial manufacturing process performs as expected.”

The EU Annex 15 [1] and PIC/S Annex 15 [33] state the following:

- “Qualification and validation activities should only be performed by suitably trained personnel who follow approved procedures.”
- “Appropriate checks should be incorporated into qualification and validation work to ensure the integrity of all data obtained.”

- “All documents generated during qualification and validation should be approved and authorised by appropriate personnel as defined in the pharmaceutical quality system.”
- “Validation protocols should be prepared which defines the critical systems, attributes and parameters and the associated acceptance criteria.”
- “Qualification documents may be combined together, where appropriate, e.g. installation qualification (IQ) and operational qualification (OQ).”
- “Results which fail to meet the pre-defined acceptance criteria should be recorded as a deviation and be fully investigated according to local procedures. Any implications for the validation should be discussed in the report.”
- “The main stages and some suggested criteria (although this depends on individual project circumstances and may be different) which could be included in each stage are indicated below:

- User requirements specification (URS)…
- Design qualification (DQ)…
- Factory acceptance testing (FAT) /Site acceptance testing (SAT)
- Equipment, especially if incorporating novel or complex technology, may be evaluated, if applicable, at the vendor prior to delivery.
- Prior to installation, equipment should be confirmed to comply with the URS/functional specification at the vendor site, if applicable.
- Where appropriate and justified, documentation review and some tests could be performed at the FAT or other stages without the need to repeat on site at IQ/OQ if it can be shown that the functionality is not affected by the transport and installation.
- FAT may be supplemented by the execution of a SAT following the receipt of equipment at the manufacturing site.
- Installation qualification (IQ)
- IQ should be performed on equipment, facilities, utilities, or systems.
- IQ should include, but is not limited to the following:
1. Verification of the correct installation of components, instrumentation, equipment, pipe work and services against the engineering drawings and specifications;
2. Verification of the correct installation against pre-defined criteria;
3. Collection and collation of supplier operating and working instructions and maintenance requirements;
4. Calibration of instrumentation;
5. Verification of the materials of construction.

# ISPE Baseline® Guide: Appendix 1

# Commissioning and Qualification

# Operational qualification (OQ)

- OQ normally follows IQ but depending on the complexity of the equipment, it may be performed as a combined Installation/Operation Qualification (IOQ).
- OQ should include but is not limited to the following:
- Tests that have been developed from the knowledge of processes, systems and equipment to ensure the system is operating as designed;
- Tests to confirm upper and lower operating limits, and /or “worst case” conditions.
- The completion of a successful OQ should allow the finalisation of standard operating and cleaning procedures, operator training and preventative maintenance requirements.

# Performance qualification (PQ)

- PQ should normally follow the successful completion of IQ and OQ. However, it may in some cases be appropriate to perform it in conjunction with OQ or Process Validation.
- PQ should include, but is not limited to the following:
- Tests, using production materials, qualified substitutes or simulated product proven to have equivalent behaviour under normal operating conditions with worst case batch sizes. The frequency of sampling used to confirm process control should be justified;
- Tests should cover the operating range of the intended process, unless documented evidence from the development phases confirming the operational ranges is available.

# 15.8 Chapter 8: Acceptance and Release

There is a regulatory expectation that equipment, facilities, utilities, and systems are accepted as being qualified prior to starting process validation (or PPQ), as described below:

- “Equipment, facilities, utilities and systems used for process validation should be qualified.” [1, 33]
- “The review and conclusions of the validation should be reported and the results obtained summarised against the acceptance criteria. Any subsequent changes to acceptance criteria should be scientifically justified and a final recommendation made as to the outcome of the validation.” [1, 33]
- “A formal release for the next stage in the qualification and validation process should be authorised by the relevant responsible personnel either as part of the validation report approval or as a separate summary document. Conditional approval to proceed to the next qualification stage can be given where certain acceptance criteria or deviations have not been fully addressed and there is a documented assessment that there is no significant impact on the next activity.” [1, 33]
- “The quality control unit must review and approve the qualification plan and report (§ 211.22).” [2]

# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

# Appendix 1

# 15.9 Chapter 9: Periodic Review

Part of the product lifecycle is ensuring that the process remains under control and that facilities, equipment, systems, and utilities remain in a qualified state for the full duration of manufacturing operations, as described below:

- “Equipment, facilities, utilities and systems should be evaluated at an appropriate frequency to confirm that they remain in a state of control.” [1, 33]
- “Where re-qualification is necessary and performed at a specific time period, the period should be justified and the criteria for evaluation defined. Furthermore, the possibility of small changes over time should be assessed.” [1, 33]
- “Once established, qualification status must be maintained through routine monitoring, maintenance, and calibration procedures and schedules (21 CFR part 211, subparts C and D). The equipment and facility qualification data should be assessed periodically to determine whether re-qualification should be performed and the extent of that re-qualification. Maintenance and calibration frequency should be adjusted based on feedback from these activities.” [2]

Regulatory references regarding periodic verification frequencies include:

- “HEPA filter integrity should be maintained to ensure aseptic conditions. Leak testing should be performed at installation to detect integrity breaches around the sealing gaskets, through the frames, or through various points on the filter media. Thereafter, leak tests should be performed at suitable time intervals for HEPA filters in the aseptic processing facility. For example, such testing should be performed twice a year for the aseptic processing room.” [23]
- “Before any sterilisation process is adopted its suitability for the product and its efficacy in achieving the desired sterilising conditions in all parts of each type of load to be processed should be demonstrated by physical measurements and by biological indicators where appropriate. The validity of the process should be verified at scheduled intervals, at least annually, and whenever significant modifications have been made to the equipment. Records should be kept of the results.” [24]
- “Process simulation tests should be performed as initial validation with three consecutive satisfactory simulation tests per shift and repeated at defined intervals and after any significant modification to the HVAC-system, equipment, process and number of shifts. Normally process simulation tests should be repeated twice a year per shift and process.” [24]

“Qualification and validation activities should only be performed by suitably trained personnel who follow approved procedures.” [1, 33]

“Where validation protocols and other documentation are supplied by a third party providing validation services, appropriate personnel at the manufacturing site should confirm suitability and compliance with internal procedures before approval. # 15.11 Chapter 11: Engineering Quality Process

The involvement of the Quality Unit in the C&Q process is detailed as follows:

- “In all stages of the product lifecycle, good project management and good archiving that capture scientific knowledge will make the process validation program more effective and efficient.” [2]
- “All documents generated during qualification and validation should be approved and authorised by appropriate personnel as defined in the pharmaceutical quality system.” [1, 33]
- “The degree and type of documentation required by CGMP vary during the validation lifecycle. Documentation requirements are greatest during Stage 2, process qualification, and Stage 3, continued process verification. Studies during these stages must conform to CGMPs and must be approved by the quality unit in accordance with the regulations (see §§ 211.22 and 211.100).” [2]
- “We recommend an integrated team approach to process validation that includes expertise from a variety of disciplines (e.g., process engineering, industrial pharmacy, analytical chemistry, microbiology, statistics, manufacturing, and quality assurance).” [2]
- “Qualification/validation personnel should report as defined in the pharmaceutical quality system although this may not necessarily be to a quality management or a quality assurance function. However, there should be appropriate quality oversight over the whole validation life cycle.” [1, 33]
- “The quality control unit must review and approve the qualification plan and report (§ 211.22).” [2]

# 15.12 Chapter 12: Change Management

Regarding change management as described in the regulatory guidance, this Guide describes it as a GEP project responsibility to track changes once the design has been reviewed and approved through the C&Q process. Once qualification is completed and process validation begins, the responsibility for change management is transferred to site QMS. The relevant regulatory texts are as follows:

- “The project plan should also include the firm’s requirements for the evaluation of changes.” [2]
- “Any significant changes to the approved protocol during execution, e.g. acceptance criteria, operating parameters etc., should be documented as a deviation and be scientifically justified.” [1, 33]
- “After establishing and confirming the process, manufacturers must maintain the process in a state of control over the life of the process, even as materials, equipment, production environment, personnel, and manufacturing procedures change.” [2]
- “The control of change is an important part of knowledge management and should be handled within the pharmaceutical quality system.” [1, 33]
- “Written procedures should be in place to describe the actions to be taken if a planned change is proposed to a starting material, product component, process, equipment, premises, product range, method of production or testing, batch size, design space or any other change during the lifecycle that may affect product quality or reproducibility.” [1, 33]

# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

# Appendix 1

- “Quality risk management should be used to evaluate planned changes to determine the potential impact on product quality, pharmaceutical quality systems, documentation, validation, regulatory status, calibration, maintenance and on any other system to avoid unintended consequences and to plan for any necessary process validation, verification or requalification efforts.” [1, 33]
- “Changes should be authorised and approved by the responsible persons or relevant functional personnel in accordance with the pharmaceutical quality system.” [1, 33]
- “There is generally a difference in formality of change management processes prior to the initial regulatory submission and after submission, where changes to the regulatory filing might be required under regional requirements.” [9]
- “Quality risk management should be utilised to evaluate proposed changes. The level of effort and formality of the evaluation should be commensurate with the level of risk.” [9]

# 15.13 Chapter 13: Good Documentation Practices for C&Q

Good documentation practices support data integrity and apply throughout the C&Q process, as described in the following regulatory texts:

- “Good documentation practices are important to support knowledge management throughout the product lifecycle.” [1, 33]
- “Generally, early process design experiments do not need to be performed under the CGMP conditions required for drugs intended for commercial distribution that are manufactured during Stage 2 (process qualification) and Stage 3 (continued process verification). They should, however, be conducted in accordance with sound scientific methods and principles, including good documentation practices.” [2]
- “The degree and type of documentation required by CGMP vary during the validation lifecycle. Documentation requirements are greatest during Stage 2, process qualification, and Stage 3, continued process verification. Studies during these stages must conform to CGMPs and must be approved by the quality unit in accordance with the regulations (see §§ 211.22 and 211.100).” [2]
- “Qualification documents may be combined together, where appropriate, e.g. installation qualification (IQ) and operational qualification (OQ).” [1, 33]
- “Any significant changes to the approved protocol during execution, e.g. acceptance criteria, operating parameters etc., should be documented as a deviation and be scientifically justified.” [1, 33]
- “Appropriate checks should be incorporated into qualification and validation work to ensure the integrity of all data obtained.” [1, 33]

Mr. Rodolfo Russo

# 15.14 Chapter 14: Implementation of Science and Risk-Based C&Q Process

Regulatory guidance highlights the importance of product quality risk management and risk assessment to determine the scope and extent of qualification as described in the following regulatory text:

- “A quality risk management approach should be applied throughout the lifecycle of a medicinal product. As part of a quality risk management system, decisions on the scope and extent of qualification and validation should be based on a justified and documented risk assessment of the facilities, equipment, utilities and processes.” [1]

# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

# Appendix 2

# 16  Appendix 2 – User Requirements Specification Example

Note: Gray text is informational only and must be deleted before document review. The template text coloration includes:

1. Gray italicized text provides directions, guidance, and suggestions on how to develop various portions of the URS document – to be removed once completed.
2. *Black italicized text in brackets* to provide sample text that may be modified to meet the needs of the system.
3. Black text is for section headings and to provide the template format that should not be changed or removed because it is language and formatting that is consistent from URS to URS.

Gray text must be deleted or changed to black. Applicable black text should remain unchanged prior to printing the document for pre-approval signatures.

Project teams must determine the need for system user requirements specification on a project-specific basis. The User Requirements Specification is a living document that serves several purposes during the lifecycle of the system:

- For new systems or equipment, the URS initiates system design and defines the basic requirements that the system must meet.
- The URS is the defining document for traceability in support of verification and qualification. The URS defines the requirements that must be met for a system to meet its intended purpose; not how the requirements are met. A system URS brings together multidisciplinary system requirements into a single document to support system design, commissioning, verification, operations and maintenance.
- Compliance to the requirements must be verifiable.
- The requirements must clearly distinguish business requirements from quality requirements.
- Critical Quality Attributes of the system product (output) and associated Critical Process Parameters must be identified in the requirements document.

This document is the basis for the design, commissioning and where appropriate verification of the system.

EXAMPLE: A filling line with multiple systems could have multiple URS documents (e.g., one URS for the Isolator, one for the Capper and one for the Filling Line if they are different vendors) or a single URS for the entire filling line.

EXAMPLE: Several identical chromatography columns could have multiple URS documents (one for each column) or a single URS for the identical columns.

EXAMPLE: Several similar chromatography columns could have multiple URS documents (one for each column) or a single URS with sections describing the different requirements for each system.

For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.

# ISPE Baseline® Guide: Appendix 2

# Commissioning and Qualification

The URS document provides input to the System Risk Assessment process, the URS may be in draft form. After the Quality Risk Assessment is the URS is updated to include CDEs identified in the risk assessment.

For modifications to existing systems, the URS may be updated to include new requirements or existing requirements may be modified to ensure that the system will reflect the process and/or system changes.

For Qualified systems the URS must be maintained in a controlled location.

For commissioned systems the URS may be stored initially in the project documentation site with the appropriate approvals. The URS for commissioned systems may then be included in the system Turnover Package (TOP) and maintained in the site engineering document repository. For ease of retrieval, a numbering format may be defined such as: Site reference – Building number – System number – Sub-system identification number.

# 1.1 URS documents must include the following sections:

# 1.1.1 Purpose and Scope:

Identify the system(s) the URS will cover.

# 1.1.2 System Overview:

Describe the system. Appropriate process flow diagrams, data flow diagrams, network diagrams, etc. may be referenced.

# 1.1.3 Definitions, Abbreviations, References:

Populate tables for definitions, abbreviations, and references as they appear and are used in the body of the URS.

# 1.1.4 Requirements:

Populate the template with information identified below.

# 1.2 Requirements must be classified as Quality or Business in the type column.

# 1.3 Include the following requirement categories. If a category is not applicable, enter “not applicable” in the template with a justification:

- Process Requirements
- Automation and records
- Design and Construction
- Operations and Maintenance
- Miscellaneous (e.g., documentation, procedural)

# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

# Appendix 2

The tables are structured as follows:

- ID Number (a unique requirement number that is auto generated in the table)
- Requirements (examples in black italicized bracketed text – good, bad, and why)
- Type: A drop down menu allowing the author to select Quality, HSE, or Business, or Other as the type of attribute
- Source (identify the source of the requirements)

In the Requirement column, specify the requirement as a condition that must be satisfied for the system to meet its intended purpose. Requirements should define and describe what is required without being prescriptive as to how the requirements are met (design solutions) unless the method of meeting the requirement is also a requirement. Requirements shall be specific and verifiable.

Use the Type column to select the type of the requirement. The Type column specifies which requirements are quality critical and which are not.

List the source of each requirement in the Source column. The source should indicate the direct source and version number. Use actual document numbers, titles, and section numbers wherever possible so that the source of the requirement is absolutely clear and traceable.

Input to the URS may come from a number of sources:

- Project Requirements Document/Charter
- Process and Product Requirements Document (PRD)
- Quality System Risk Assessment
- National, local and site requirements
- Legacy equipment evaluations
- SME input

Source documents do not need to be approved to be used as references. Changes and revisions to these source documents may require revision to the URS.

The columns in the table have example text showing both appropriate and inappropriate requirements.

If a section is not used, delete the table but retain the section header and add “Not applicable.” after the text. For example, if the URS contained no physical product properties, the table in section 3.2 would be deleted and the section title changed to: “3.2 Process Requirements – Physical Product Properties – N/A”

Subject Matter Experts (SME) should establish their requirements through the project documentation such as the PRD.

# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

# Appendix 3

# 17  Appendix 3 – System Classification Form

Example

System Impact Evaluation (SIE) Document No.: &lt;SIE-XXXXX&gt;

&lt;Manufacturing Area&gt; Issue Date: &lt;DD-MMM-YYYY&gt;

|Number|Impact Evaluation Criteria Question|
|---|---|
|Q1|Does the system contain CAs/CDEs or perform functions that serve to meet one or more process requirements (CQAs) including CPPs? Provide a comment if the Yes answer is the result of interaction with a process control/automation system within the system boundary.|
|Q2|Does the system have direct contact with the product or process stream and such contact have the potential to impact the final product quality or pose a risk to the patient?|
|Q3|Does the system provide an excipient or produce an ingredient or solvent (e.g., WFI) and could the quality (and compliance with the required specifications thereof) of this substance impact the final product quality or pose a risk to the patient?|
|Q4|Is the system used in cleaning, sanitizing, or sterilizing, and could malfunction of the system result in failure to adequately clean, sanitize, or sterilize such that a risk to the patient would result?|
|Q5|Does the system establish a proper environment (e.g., nitrogen blanket, closed process, exposed filling zone air quality, maintenance of temperature, humidity, when such parameter is part of the product CPPs) for the process and could failure of the system to function properly pose a risk to the patient?|
|Q6|Does the system use, produce, process, or store data used to accept or reject product, CPPs, or electronic records subject to 21 CFR Part 11 and EU GMP Vol. 4, Annex 11 or the local equivalent?|
|Q7|Does the system provide container closure or product protection, the failure of which would pose a risk to the patient or degradation of product quality?|
|Q8|Does the system provide product identification information (e.g., lot number, expiration date, counterfeit prevention features) without independent verification or is the system used to verify this information?|

If any of the answers to questions 1 – 8 are “Yes”, the system shall be classified as a “Direct Impact” System.

Q9 Was Question 1 answered as Yes due to interaction with a process control/automation system included within the system boundary and/or Question 6? If Yes, a GAMP Categorization is required.

If all of the answers to questions 1 – 8 are “No” the system shall be classified as a “Not Direct Impact” System. “Not direct impact” systems do not need further quality assessment but must be managed through Good Engineering Practices.

# Appendix 4

# Appendix 4 – Direct Impact System Examples

Note: This set of examples is provided for illustrative purposes. The CQAs and CPPs associated with each system type may differ for a specific product and process.

|System Name|Typical CQAs|Typical CPPs|
|---|---|---|
|Autoclave System|• Sterile load (microbial levels reduced by ≥ log 6) (SAL ≥ 10⁻⁶) • Load dryness Note: Steam quality is typically verified as part of the steam system|• Clean steam pressure and temperature (saturation) • Time • Vacuum level • Defined load pattern|
|Blending System|• Blend uniformity (potency)|• Number of revolutions • Rotational time|
|Buffer Hold System|• pH • Conductivity • Bioburden • Endotoxin|• Temperature (for buffer stability/microbial control) • Weight/level (if not able to leverage unit operation readings)|
|Buffer Preparation System|• pH • Conductivity • Bioburden • Endotoxin|• Temperature (for proper dissolution) • Weight/level and/or flow (for proper batching quantities) • Most prep systems do not include, but if so: - In situ conductivity probe - In situ pH probe • Agitation rate, if critical for buffer preparation (not typical)|
|Capper RABS System|• Supply air quality|• Total particle count • Viable particle count • Air velocity|
|Capping System|• Container closure integrity|• Placing closure into container accurately • Maintaining closure pressure when capping • Torque (for screw cap)|
|Centrifuge System|• Mr. Rodolfo Russo Harvest pool titer|• Bowl speed • Feed flow rate • Back pressure • Discharge interval (if not based on feed flow or turbidity) • Turbidity (if used to trigger discharges)|
|Chromatography Column System (Process)|• Product specific analytical data (can be measured using PAT or off-line) • Protein concentration • Bioburden • Endotoxin|• Bed height • Bed density|

|Filter Press System|• Step yield|• Pressure|
|Filter/Filter Housing System|• Protein concentration and/or step yield • Bioburden and/or viral assay (based on purpose of the filter step)|• Differential pressure across filters|
|Filtration System|• Protein concentration and/or step yield|• Flow • Differential pressure across filters|
|Filtration System: Alternating Tangential Flow (ATF)|• Protein concentration in bioreactor • Viable cell density in bioreactor|• Permeate flow rate • Vacuum/pressure to ATF pump|
|Filtration System: Ultrafiltration/Diafiltration (UF/DF)|• Protein concentration • Yield • Bioburden|• Flow • Vessel level/weight • Permeate UV • Endotoxin • Transmembrane pressure|
|Formulation System|• Uniformity|• Time • Mixer speed|
|Harvest Collection System|• RHO, MILANO • Bioburden (for cell culture processes)|• Temperature (pool stability/microbial control) • Agitation • Vessel pH (if used for control/titration)|
|Harvest Surge and Clarification System|• Protein concentration and/or step yield • Bioburden|• Differential pressure across filters • Agitation • Flow rate • Temperature|

# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

# Appendix 5

# 19  Appendix 5 – System Risk Assessment

# Example

# Process Air System

The process air system is direct impact and provides compressed air to meet predefined quality specifications. Note
that this type of system is typically industry standard and the air generation associated equipment is commissioned
only (not qualified) since the quality requirements are either verified during start-up (e.g., hydrocarbon content) or
monitored where there is a risk of them not being met through use of the system (e.g., pressure dew point of the air
from the dryer, total and viable particle count from critical point of use).

# Operations Sequence/Process Flow

The start-up and shutdown conditions are not considered important from a quality perspective, only the in-operation
state hence this has been considered.

# CQAs:

- Moisture content
- Hydrocarbon content
- Total particle count
- Viable particle count
- Capacity (volume/pressure/flow)

# CPPs:

- Dryer settings (but dryer is a commissioned system with output continuously monitored for the CQA)
- Pressure/flow (but air generation is a commissioned system with output continuously monitored for the CQA)
- Air classification (if POU is installed in a classified environment)

# CDEs/Procedures:

- Post-dryer commercial filters Mr. Rodolfo Russo
- Post-dryer pharmaceutical filter RHO, MILANO
- Filter change procedure ID number: 364201
- Humidity (dew point) monitoring and alarm, instrument specification/calibration
- Pressure/flow monitoring instrument specification/calibration
- Hydrocarbon content (depends on the design approach, i.e., oil free compressor of hydrocarbon removal by
filtration)

# ISPE Baseline® Guide: Appendix 5 Commissioning and Qualification

The System Risk Assessment should align the sequence of operations/process flow with the CQAs and their associated CPPs, as shown for this system in Table 19.1. The baseline System Risk Assessment and alarm classification for this example are shown in Table 19.2 and 19.3.

# Table 19.1: Process Air System Example: Critical Quality Attributes and Critical Process Parameters Derived from the URS

|Reference Number|Operations Sequence|Process Description|Critical Quality Attribute (CQA)|Critical Process Parameter (CPP)|
|---|---|---|---|---|
|1|Pre-filter|Pre-filter to protect compressor mechanisms|N/A|N/A|
|2|Compressor|Compresses air (in the process concentrating particulate contamination and increasing relative humidity)|Capacity|Volume/pressure/flow|
|3|Filter/separator|Allows condensate to drain and removes coarse particulates|N/A|N/A|
|4|Commercial filter|Remove particulates and hydrocarbon|N/A|N/A|
|5|Dryer|Dries air to prevent any condensation forming in use and to limit microbial contamination|Moisture content|Dryer settings (but dryer is a commissioned system with output continuously monitored for the CQA)|
|6|Commercial filter/separator|Retains any particulate shedding from the dryer desiccant|N/A|N/A|
|7|Receiver|Provides “surge storage” to the system supplying short periods of high demand, without system pressure drop|N/A|N/A|
|8|Commercial filter|Provides particulate and hydrocarbon levels|Particulate total, viable; hydrocarbon level|Filter change frequency|
|9|Final filter (typically pharmaceutical grade)|Polices particulate and hydrocarbon levels|Secondary|Policing filter, i.e., no active duty unless prior filters fail|

Note: During operation, flow and pressure will vary throughout the distribution system; a typical approach used is not to consider system flow/pressure as critical, but to ensure every direct impact system fed from the system where air pressure (flow) is critical has a low pressure switch that is considered a critical instrument, with an appropriate function defined if low pressure is detected.

# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

# Appendix 6

# Capital Project Name:

&lt;[Project Stage] GMP Design Review&gt; – Meeting Minutes

# Detailed Meeting Agenda Items for GMP Design Review Meetings

The concept GMP design review is anticipated to be a high-level review of the concept package against the user requirements and GMP expectations in the regulatory design basis, quality guidelines, and engineering design standards. It is important to obtain consensus on major approaches and decisions to avoid later rework. The meeting agenda list is not meant to be all inclusive and items may need to be added or deleted based on the project scope and type. Items that should be covered include:

# Concept GMP Design Review

1. General Project GMP Requirements
- Overall project scope and business case requirements
- Project type
- Regulatory design basis review
2. Facility Layout and Material Handling
- Overall facility layout
- Room classification/hygienic zoning schemes
- Product, people, material, and waste flow
- Transitions requirements
- Gowning levels
- Cold rooms, freezer rooms, and incubator rooms (locations and requirements)
3. Process and Operations
- Overall process summary
- Identification of sterile process steps
- Overall equipment cleaning philosophy
- Review of product quality risk assessment including risk of contamination from (1) particulates internal and external to the equipment, (2) microorganisms (for sterile products), and (3) other products or residues (i.e., cross-contamination)
4. HVAC
- Room pressurization
- Air handling unit zoning and segregation
- Temperature and relative humidity requirements
- Filtration
5. Support Functions
- Identification of approach for required support functions including Quality Operations labs, in-process test labs, warehouse space, sampling and dispensing areas, and offices impacting manufacturing areas

Mr. Rodolfo Russo
6. Automation and Documentation Systems
- Automation strategy
- Critical parameter monitoring approach
- Building Automation System

Date: &lt;DD-MMM-YYYY&gt;

Page 5 of &lt;X&gt;

For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
---
# ISPE Baseline® Guide: Appendix 6

# Commissioning and Qualification

# Capital Project Name:

&lt;[Project Stage] GMP Design Review&gt; – Meeting Minutes

# G. Utilities and Process Support Systems

- Vacuum
- Compressed air/nitrogen/process gases
- Clean steam
- CIP/SIP
- Dust Collection
- Waste treatment and process drainage systems

# H. Purified Water/WFI Systems

- System overview

# I. Miscellaneous

- Project-specific items that may impact GMP aspects of the facility

# Basis of Design (BOD) GMP Design Review

The BOD GMP Review should concentrate on resolution of open items from the concept review, changes to the design from concept and the additional design detail developed. The meeting agenda list may be similar to the concept meeting agenda with review of open items and changes from concept meeting and review of the additional documentation developed including the validation master plan, system classification (direct impact or not direct impact), and project change control philosophy.

Additionally, the BOD GMP Review should have sufficient detail to present issues concerning licensing of the new facility, impact to any ongoing operations (if applicable), and relevant phasing of the project with regard to the initial schedule for the project.

# 90% Detail Design GMP Design Review

The 90% detail design GMP design review is intended to be a detailed review of the GMP aspects of the design. The major topics to be covered in 90% detail design GMP design review meetings are listed below. Recommended items to cover under each topic are also listed. The items listed are not all inclusive. Items may be deleted or added depending on the type of project and GMP requirements specific to the project scope.

# 90% Detail Design GMP Review Meeting

# A. General Project GMP Requirements

- This Document is licensed to Overall project scope and business case requirements
- Changes from BOD GMP Design Review
- Resolution of open items from BOD GMP Design Review
- Review of updated Regulatory Design Basis including:

# Design Qualification Process Summary

Design Qualification (DQ) Objective: Establish that the design of the facility and equipment takes GMP into account and that the facility and equipment is designed to be suitable for intended purpose. Equipment DQ establishes that the facility and equipment is adequately designed to reliably produce product meeting established quality specifications in accordance with GMPs.

Insert summary of activities performed for Design Qualification of facilities and equipment. The following is an example of a DQ process summary. The summary should be adjusted to match actual project activities.

The Design Qualification process has included the following activities:

- Cross-functional involvement of SMEs (i.e., Quality, Engineering, Technology, and Operations) throughout the design process.
- Identification and adherence to applicable company policies, guides and standards. No variances from the guides and standards are anticipated for this project.
- Development of the Regulatory Design Basis.
- Development of the Validation Master Plan or commissioning/qualification/validation equivalent document.
- Performed System Classification (direct impact or not direct impact).
- Performed System Risk/Criticality Assessments (i.e., risk assessment for C&Q).
- Performed a product quality risk assessment including risk of contamination from (1) particulates, (2) microorganisms (for sterile products), and other products or residues (i.e., cross-contamination).
- Developed User Requirements and Engineering Specifications for direct impact GMP equipment.
- Developed list of CAs/CDEs and associated acceptance criteria.
- Held formal equipment GMP review meetings for all direct impact equipment to verify that the critical aspects of the equipment have been incorporated in the equipment design. Meetings have been documented in meeting minutes.
- Held structured cross-functional GMP Design Review meetings at end of concept and BOD and detail design. Meetings were documented with meeting minutes. Open items and their resolutions have been documented.

Design will be verified through planned commissioning and qualification inspection and testing, in accordance with the Validation Master Plan or equivalent document.

# Conclusion – DQ Criteria were met:

- Design satisfies GMP and regulatory requirements.
- Design meets User Requirements.
- Design controls described in the System Risk Assessments have been incorporated into the design.
- DQ process complies with company quality guidelines and engineering standards and procedures.
- Recommend approval of DQ declaration.

# Appendix 7 – Supporting Plans

Depending on the size of a project and the resources used, the following supporting plans may be developed to ensure a common understanding of the work required:

- Construction Quality Plan (CQP): The construction team should develop a project-specific CQP that incorporates the facility owner’s requirements and procedures with those of the construction leader. Quality expectations to be supported by designers, fabricators, and vendors should be clearly communicated and documented in specifications, plans, and contractual commitment documents. The owner’s requirements should also reflect C&Q requirements. The CQP should be developed prior to starting the procurement process and should be included in each applicable bid package. Aspects which should be addressed in a CQP include, but are not limited to:

# ISPE Baseline® Guide: Appendix 7

# Commissioning and Qualification

- C&Q Safety Plan: The project team should develop a safety plan which is specific to the C&Q activities. Safety hazard analysis reviews are performed to identify activities that have higher than normal risk to employees and property. Specific plans, procedures, and training should be implemented to address the identified hazards.

Other plans that may be useful for larger projects include:

- Cleanliness Plan: Describes how equipment will be stored and cleaned prior to installation.
- Project Plan: An overarching plan describing all of the project deliverables, roles and responsibilities, and defining all of the other supporting plans.
- Procurement Plan: Defines the vendor assessment process, roles and responsibilities, schedule, and progress monitoring/reporting.
- Construction Logistics Plan: Identifies locations for items such as material/equipment storage, laydown, staging, temporary offices, dumpsters, break/lunch areas, and temporary utilities. Methods of maintaining and monitoring GMP conditions of existing operations and support areas during demolition and construction should be established as part of this plan.

# Discrepancy Description:

Include a complete description of the discrepancy including the specific acceptance criteria that was not met, actual result, and any information required to connect this discrepancy to the associated document verification or test (e.g., test date or run number). This should not include justification or investigation elements.

# Discrepancy Resolution:

Include the justification or investigation and resulting root cause with any supporting data attached. Corrective actions will be detailed and followed to completion within this document. A statement about the status of testing or any retesting requirements should also be detailed or include a clear statement that the discrepancy is closed.

# Corrective Action Implementation Results (as applicable):

Document that corrective actions have been implemented, any retesting results, and a clear statement that the discrepancy is closed. If retesting results in additional failures, a new discrepancy should be opened.

# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Appendix 12

# 26 Appendix 12 – Periodic Review for Controlled Temperature Chambers

This appendix describes an approach to periodic review of Controlled Temperature Chambers (CTCs), recognizing the value of the data provided by the independent monitoring system and the quality systems used to support normal operation, including:

- Change control
- Deviations
- Regular review of the system performance data

For the purposes of this chapter, the term CTCs:

- Includes:
- Purchased standard catalog items such as freezers, refrigerators, warming cabinets, incubators, and walk-in warm rooms (designed to maintain consistent temperatures)
- Custom-built units such as controlled room temperature environments (used for material storage), walk-in cold rooms, and freezers operating under GMP
- Does not include:
- Systems used for processing, i.e., heating or cooling

The periodic review approach for CTCs proposed in this appendix involves assigning each system a category. The periodic review is then scheduled based on the category.

Periodic review needs to be completed prior to the assigned due date; failure to meet the schedule is considered a non-conformity. The schedule may be revised in the following situations:

- The CTC is removed from service
- There is a change requiring requalification of the CTC

# 26.1 Assignment of Categories and Periodic Review Schedule Requirements

CTCs are categorized (CTC Category 1, 2, or 3) for the purposes of periodic review by evaluating the material stored and the system operating temperature range:

Materials Stored: stored materials are grouped according to the potential risk to finished drug product quality as follows:

- Stored Material Type A: Drug substance, drug product, stability sample(s), lab samples, cool/hot packs used to maintain temperature during transportation (if cool/hot pack temperature is not verified before use)
- Stored Material Type B: Work in Progress (WIP), reference standards, critical reagents, buffers, raw materials

For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
---
# ISPE Baseline® Guide: Appendix 12 Commissioning and Qualification

Stored Material Type C: Environmental Monitoring (EM) supplies and reagents, media, test materials

# Operating Temperature Range:

- For a CTC with a relatively narrow operating range (≤ 6°C), e.g., 2–8°C, 2–4°C, there is a higher risk of a temperature excursion through non-major failures such as drift.
- For a CTC with a broader operating range (> 6°C), a failure would most likely be caused by significant mechanical failure which is highly detectable.

Table 26.1 provides an example CTC category assignment approach and Table 26.2 details the associated periodic review frequencies for the categories.

**Table 26.1: CTC Category Assignment Based on Materials Stored and Operating Temperature Range**
|Stored Material Type|Precision Unit (operating temperature range is ≤ 6°C, e.g., 2–8°C)|Standard Unit: Temperature Range > 6°C|Conditioned Room: Controlled Room Temperature*|
|---|---|---|---|
|A|1|2|2|
|B|2|2|3|
|C|2|3|3|

*Per USP &lt;659&gt; [35]: “Controlled room temperature: the temperature maintained thermostatically that encompasses the usual and customary working environment of 20°-25°C (68°-77°F). The following conditions also apply. Mean kinetic temperature not to exceed 25°C. Excursions between 15°C and 30°C (59° and 86°F) that are experienced in pharmacies, hospitals, and warehouses, and during shipping are allowed. Provided the mean kinetic temperature does not exceed 25°C, transient spikes up to 40º are permitted as long as they do not exceed 24 h[ours]. Spikes above 40° may be permitted only if the manufacturer so instructs.”

**Table 26.2: CTC Periodic Review Scheduling Requirement**
|CTC Category|Periodic Review Scheduling Requirement|
|---|---|
|1|Periodic review not to exceed three years from the previous review|
|2|Periodic review of system performance obtained through review of monitoring probe(s) data|
|3|System performance verification and periodic review is not required|

- System performance verification for CTC Category 2 units should be demonstrated through quarterly review of monitoring probe(s) data by appropriate SME(s).
- Liquid Nitrogen (LN2) freezers do not require periodic review or system performance verification since operation is controlled by the laws of physics.

# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

# Appendix 12

# 26.2 Periodic Review Assessment

The periodic review assessment approach described in this appendix considers risks to the system performance. A template is provided in Section 26.3 for performing the review.

The template should be revised to reflect the details and findings for the CTC under review:

- Identify the system or sub-system(s) being assessed
- Identify the start and end date of the review period
- Record the results of the review including the location for the source of the information and attaching copies where appropriate
- Define any actions required after review of the information

The report summary needs to state if the system is considered suitable for ongoing use.

If the periodic review produces results that are not considered by the SME to demonstrate that the system is suitable for the intended purpose, the SME should recommend appropriate actions and evaluate if a non-conformity is required.

Recommended actions may include:

- Performing remapping studies to confirm temperature ranges/distribution
- Reviewing data to confirm the location of the monitoring probe(s) or to assess if the monitoring probe(s) location(s) need to be changed
- Repair/adjustment of the temperature control system

# 26.3 Periodic Review Report Template

The template should be edited to suit the specific CTC or group of CTCs. Black text is intended to be left but can be edited as needed to improve context or clarify content. Gray italic text provides instructions/guidance or suggested text that must be deleted/revised and turned into the standard font before sending for approval.

Note: All gray italic text must be deleted before sending the document for approval.

The final draft is to be post-execution approved by the system owner or delegate and Quality.

# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

# Appendix 13

# Appendix 13 – Vendor Assessment Tool

Example

The following is an example of an assessment format.

Methodology: The assessment will be done by reviewing each of the following areas. The reviews will include a discussion on the respective topics. Review and verification of the applicable procedures and practices will be documented. This will include a spot check of the respective records as proof of verification. Checks from procedures to floor and floor to procedures will be done as part of the assessment. A copy of these requirements is to be sent and communicated to the vendor before the site visit.

1. Quality System Overview: Perform an overview of the Quality Management System (QMS) to get an understanding that a documented system exists and is in use. Document the overview review of the vendor QMS and attach any relevant copies of vendor QMS documents. Focus on those elements of the QMS that impact understanding of the integrity of the data which will be collected over time or as projects progress.
2. Employee Training: How does the vendor train employees in the following?
1. Development of test documents (vendor internal testing, FAT, start-up, commissioning, or qualification) that are specific to the system. Verify by records review.
2. Performing verification, FAT, or qualification testing for owners both in the factory and at the owner site. Verify by records review.
3. Documentation of test exceptions, failures, corrections, and retests when performing formal tests for owners. Verify by records review.
4. Good Documentation Practice – verify by records review.
3. Design Controls: How does the vendor control the design process (i.e., engineering change management), manage and document changes? Review the respective SOP(s) and records as applicable.
4. Support Documentation: How are the vendor document packages specific to this build?
1. Review of typical or actual vendor support documentation packages and qualification
2. This Document is licensed to
3. Is documentation developed concurrently with design and fabrication activities?
5. Materials of Construction: How does the vendor provide traceability on critical components and materials of construction?
6. Employee Turnover: How does the vendor handle employee turnover during a build of a system for a client?
7. Employee Certifications and Training: Provide evidence of employee experience, certifications and training (employee qualifications).
8. Testing Instruments: Provide evidence of vendor instrument calibration and certifications traceable to NIST or equivalent standards.
9. Project Staffing: Provide evidence of which employees performed the respective build, inspection, testing, and documentation.

# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

# Appendix 15

# 29 Appendix 15 – Approach to Qualifying Legacy Systems or Systems with Inadequate Qualification

# 29.1 Introduction

The legacy system situation occurs when a system that has an impact on product quality is determined to have not been qualified to meet current expectations. With upgrade and retrofit projects, there is often the concern that if a new effort is to be made to address this gap in content, any previous documentation should be updated to reflect the more recent qualification efforts. This should not be a concern as long as the respective documents conform to the procedures that were effective at the time.

For many existing facilities, the supporting documents that were previously employed to demonstrate suitability for the intended purpose may have followed a different approach, using engineering specifications as the primary basis for acceptance criteria. With legacy systems, there are often limited user requirement documents and design phase deliverables (e.g., URS and DR). Instead of retrospectively generating the specification and design phase deliverables, the focus of a more efficient effort should be on identifying the product and process user requirements and assessing risks to the system product quality.

Process user requirements should be well documented including CQAs and CPPs. Where this is not the case, a multi-disciplinary team should collaborate to define and document these requirements. For a legacy manufacturing facility, the batch record data and process/product failures may be useful in determining the process user requirements. Review deviation records to see if the system has been identified as cause of product quality failures.

The next step in addressing legacy systems is to conduct an identification of the CDEs using risk to product quality as the underlying criteria. This is especially useful to narrow the focus of the legacy system qualification effort. Once the CDEs have been defined, the available test documentation package should be assessed to determine that the CDEs have been shown to function correctly. For most legacy systems, there are system performance data, calibration records, and maintenance records that can be useful in evaluating the system performance.

It is necessary to confirm that accurate drawings exist for the as-installed system, data in the maintenance files are up-to-date to support operation/maintenance, and test evidence supports any changes to the system since it was put into service.

In summary, the objectives of the legacy project should include:

- Defining the product and process user requirements
- Assessing risks to the product quality:
- Identifying the hazards and associated CDE based on risk to CQA or CPP
- Verifying that the CDEs have been properly tested and are being properly calibrated, maintained, and operate as necessary to control these risks to product quality
- Ensuring accurate drawings and equipment files exist to support the continued operation, maintenance, and change records to equipment, systems, and automation controls (software/hardware)

For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
---
# ISPE Baseline® Guide: Appendix 15

# Commissioning and Qualification

- Reviewing overall equipment and process operational history and address any recurring problems

These deliverables should be documented and approved by the Quality Unit.

# 29.2 Qualification of Legacy Systems

Qualification of legacy systems following the normal process may not be feasible as most of the documents typically used to support the process may not be retrievable (e.g., purchase orders, equipment quotations). A risk-based approach, as described in this section, can be used.

Each system or system family (where there are multiple systems of the same type) is subject to a risk assessment by a team consisting of:

- SME (typically the system owner or delegate)
- Facilitator
- Other SMEs that are identified as necessary by the system owner to understand the system and controls together
- Quality Unit representative

The team identifies aspects of the system installation or operation that could impact product quality. To make the process more effective, the SMEs may develop a draft for review by the team with a focused group.

The risk controls expected to be in place should be identified and the risk level assessed. Low or medium risks are considered acceptable; high risks are addressed by adding additional controls.

Risk controls may be technical controls or procedural controls; examples include:

- Technical controls
- Reject mechanisms
- Temperature control loops, interlocks, and alarms
- Procedural controls
- Operational SOPs
- Maintenance and calibration requirements
- Action in the event of an alarm
- In-process tests

The risk controls should be investigated and confirmation obtained that they are in place, thus the system is considered suitable for the intended purpose. The report should be signed off by all participants in the assessment.

The system should be listed in the periodic review program. Any issues experienced with the system, e.g., deviations, would trigger a review of the report. If gaps are found, then they should be listed in the report. The report should be signed off with a cross reference to the tracking system. All gaps should be transferred to this system and progressed to completion. On completion, the system should be transferred into the periodic review system.

For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
---
# ISPE Baseline® Guide:

# Commissioning and Qualification

# Appendix 15

# 29.3 The System Qualification Risk Assessment Process for Legacy Systems

The risk assessment identifies the CPPs for the system, considering the system product CQAs including purity, identity, strength, and the associated CPPs and CDEs.

- Purity
- Materials of construction could impact product quality
- For coded vessels, materials of construction are confirmed through documentation
- For non-coded vessels, visual inspection of the vessel may be deemed acceptable to confirm that there is no evidence of degradation, or a non-destructive test can be carried out.
- Non-metallic materials (e.g., gaskets) should be defined in maintenance procedures and routinely changed
- Vessel integrity (i.e., whether it can be maintained at pressure/vacuum) could impact product quality if there is ingress of contaminants
- Contamination
- System is CIP’d and SIP’d (cleaning validation and temperature mapping are performed independently)
- Identity – N/A (in the scope of cleaning validation)
- Strength
- System maintains product temperature – as verified by calibrated sensor
- System mixes materials, mixer is effective – engineering study/process development process qualification/in-process testing
- General Controls
- As-built record drawing is available
- Maintenance and calibration procedures (including a spare parts list) are available; SME review to confirm that they are appropriate
- Operational procedures are available; SME review to confirm that they are appropriate, including, e.g., visual inspection before use to ensure the vessel is clean and check of instrument calibration dates before use.

# 29.4 Making a Minor Change to a Compliant Legacy System

ID number: 364201

A risk assessment of the change or review of an existing risk assessment for an equivalent system can be used to determine if the change impacts any of the risk controls for the system and would require qualification or if it is just an engineering change. The supporting documentation should be developed following the principles described in this Guide.

The scope of the risk review should be limited to the scope of the change. There is no requirement to modify the existing documentation to meet the new requirements (e.g., to requalify the complete system) when a minor change has been made.

Downloaded on: 7/18/20 2:03 AM

For individual use only. © Copyright ISPE 2019. All rights reserved.
---
# ISPE Baseline® Guide: Appendix 15

# Commissioning and Qualification

When making changes to previously validated systems, if the documentation is considered acceptable, then the updating of the legacy documentation may be the most efficient way to document this change. If a legacy deliverable (e.g., Component Criticality Assessment) is in place, this should also be updated as required by the change to maintain a current qualification package.

# 29.5 Unknown Product/Process Requirements

For some types of projects, the product and/or process user requirements may not be known, whether completely or partially. This situation can occur for projects such as:

- Where product or process development work is ongoing
- Multi-product facilities, contract manufacturing facilities, or other situations where the product to be manufactured has not yet been identified
- Research and development facilities
- Clinical manufacturing facilities

There needs to be some basis for the design. For most projects, the user is able to define a generic set of process requirements and/or performance capabilities that the system should meet. These requirements become the basis for design and can serve as the process requirements. For some facilities, there may also be some general requirements that are derived from regulatory expectations, such as the expectation that aseptic filling will occur under ISO 5/Grade A conditions with unidirectional airflow.

System Risk Assessments and DRs can be performed. Individuals with expertise in the particular manufacturing process technology should be included in the team. The risk assessment should progress as normal, identifying CDEs that serve to control product quality risk.

Verification work is based on engineering specifications and carried out by SMEs. The verification work should include performance tests and determination of equipment operating capabilities (ranges, control accuracy, etc.) so that future product and process requirements can be readily evaluated against the capabilities of the process equipment and systems.

In many cases, it may be appropriate to evaluate the equipment suitability in the following terms:

- Confirming the assessed risks to product quality are adequately controlled
- Defining equipment performance capabilities acceptable to the user – since there are no defined product/process user requirements other than generic requirements used as the basis for design, the acceptance criteria becomes what is acceptable and agreed on by the user and process SME

Once development work is complete and product/process requirements are finalized, it is important that the project team confirms and documents that the generic product/process requirements that were originally defined are aligned with the final product/process requirements. If any aspects of the defined product/process are outside of the system qualified state, then the necessary changes can be made under change control and qualified.

Mr. Rodolfo Russo

RHO, MILANO

# 31.2 Definitions

# Acceptance Criteria (WHO Annex 5 [13])

Measurable terms under which a test result may be considered acceptable.

# Commissioning Plan

A strategy and plan document that defines the facilities, systems, and equipment that will be commissioned, based on the established system boundaries, and the scope of the commissioning effort. The plan should include the commissioning strategy, responsibilities, and expectations for the project.

# Commissioning

A well-planned, documented, and managed engineering approach to the start-up and turnover of facilities, systems, utilities, and equipment to the end-user that results in a safe and functional environment that meets established design requirements and stakeholder expectations.

This Document is licensed to Mr. Rodolfo Russo RHO, MILANO
